Catalog
Toh 831 (Tōhoku catalogue; also cited as D 831 of the Derge edition) · Derge Kangyur · Old Tantras (rnying rgyud) · Tantra

About this text

This text is known in English as the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, or more fully the Great Vehicle Sūtra called the Essence of the Meaning of the Secret Wisdom of the Heart of All the Tathāgatas, the Sūtra of the Awareness That Gathers All the Wrathful Vajra Families, the Tantra of Accomplished Yoga; its Sanskrit title is given as Sarvatathāgatacittaguhyajñānārthagarbha Krodhavajrakulatantra Piṇḍārtha Vidyāyogasiddha, and in Tibetan it begins De zhin shek pa tam ché kyi tuk sang wé yeshé dön gyi nyingpo, the Wrathful Vajra Family All-Gathering Awareness Sūtra. Although it calls itself a sūtra, it is a tantra of the Ancient tradition, set in a visionary assembly where the Blessed One, glorious Samantabhadra in the form of a wrathful heruka and lord of the supreme secret, teaches the secret meaning of the wrathful vajra families. Across its thirty-three chapters it sets out the view, practice and ritual of this secret path, and its closing chapters describe in detail the vajra empowerment, with its royal emblems, mantras and conditions for a worthy disciple, before ending in the assembly's song of praise to the tantra as a mirror of all phenomena. It is Toh 831 in the Kangyur, where it belongs to the section of Ancient (Nyingma) Tantras, among the scriptures the Nyingma tradition associates with its higher classes of inner tantra.

Translation

In the language of India: sarvatathāgatacittaguhyajñānaarthagarbhe krodhavajrakulatantra piṇḍārtha vidyāyogasiddha nāma mahāyānasūtra

In the language of Tibet: The Great Vehicle Sūtra called the Essence of the Meaning of the Secret Wisdom of the Heart of All the Tathāgatas, the Sūtra of the Awareness That Gathers All the Wrathful Vajra Families, the Tantra of Accomplished Yoga.

Homage to the Blessed One, the glorious Liberator, the King of Compassion.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One of all the tathāgatas, glorious Samantabhadra, Vajra Saṅa Heruka, lord of the supreme secret, sovereign of blessing, the array of the inexhaustible treasury, who moves with sublime conduct for the great purpose, was dwelling in the place where this very marvelous meaning is taught. That place is the secret of secrets, the palace of the dharmadhātu (the expanse of phenomena) that is the ground of all, the expanse where the drops of light are spread out. It is the celestial mansion of primordial vastness, marvelously renowned, unbounded in its extent in every direction. There, from the sky of dharmatā, clouds of miracles gather everywhere and the rain of awakening pours down; the torment of the afflictions is cooled, and the shoots of wisdom spring forth. It is without edge or center, its pinnacles everywhere equal in extent, adorned with the king of jewels, and beautiful with strands of pearls, hanging tassels, and pendants. It is lit by the great sun, inlaid by the great moon, and studded with the stars and the brightest stars, in a sky made radiant all around by the light of vajra jewels.

There a mass of black wind spreads evenly, unbounded in its extent; a mass of blood surges in billowing waves, unbounded in its extent in every direction; there are places of outcastes and raised mounds; and upon a ground covered evenly in every direction with heaps of bones stands a blazing celestial palace, radiating light everywhere. Its walls are of stacked skulls, with nails of meteoric iron driven in tightly. On all its rafters, suns and moons, risen and not yet risen, swing to and fro. Its great beams are great fish, crammed with small fish. Its column bases are moons, its column capitals suns, its column ornaments are garlands of serpents, and so forth. Thunderbolts of hūṃ and a rain of fire pour down. It is adorned with canopies of dharmatā, hung with pendants of wisdom, and covered with vajra ornaments and banners. In all the spaces between, sloping down with fringes of black snakes and eaves like the shadows of black clouds, it is beautiful with jewels, pearls, and flowers. The maṇḍala of saṃsāra is spread out there, adorned all around with whole corpses large and small, dried carcasses upper and lower, limbs long and short, garlands of sense organs, and vajras, and it is illuminated all around by nine gates.

In that place there were gathered the Vajra of Aversion called Vajra Regent, the Jewel of Pride called Vajra Gophur, the Lotus of Desire called Vajra Kiṃkara, the Variegated One of Jealousy called Vajra Karma, the Holder of the Signs of Warmth called Vajra Crow, the Attendant of Harmonious Engagement called Vajra Juncture of Time, all the vajra siblings, all the vajra names, all the vajra vidyādharas, all the vajra bodies, all the vajra yogas, all the vajra speech, all the vajra actions, all the vajra minds, all the vajra mothers, all the vajra kiṃkaras, all the vajra ḍākas, all the vajra swift-goers, all the vajra sorceresses, all the vajra all-blazing women, all the vajra yakṣas, all the vajra rākṣasīs, all the women such as the vajra household women, courtesans, and servant women, and all the vajra wrathful ones such as the vajra envoys. Together with all the buddhas of the vajra family, he was dwelling majestically and without fear upon the vajra seat, together with a great retinue, namely:

The buddha called Great Miracle, Lord of Space; the buddha called Great Miracle, Bestower of Liberation; the buddha called Great Miracle, Proclaimer of Liberation; the buddha called Great Miracle, All-Accomplishing; the great yoginī called Daughter of Space, the Powerful Lady; the great yoginī called Daughter of Space, Gatherer of Joy; the great yoginī called Daughter of Space, the Tormentress; the great yoginī called Daughter of Space, Tārā; and the others, the retinue of the utterly great secret.

And further, the retinue of the great samaya, namely: the buddha called Great Samaya, Vajra Essence; the buddha called Great Samaya, Jewel Essence; the buddha called Great Samaya, Lotus Essence; the buddha called Great Samaya, Essence of the Array; the great yoginī called Great Samaya, Sovereign Lady of the Vajra; the great yoginī called Great Samaya, Sovereign Lady of the Jewel; the great yoginī called Great Samaya, Sovereign Lady of the Lotus; the great yoginī called Great Samaya, Sovereign Lady of Ornaments; and the others, the retinue of the great, utterly inviolable.

And further, the retinue of the immeasurables, namely: the buddha called Great Secret, Essence of Loving-Kindness; the buddha called Great Secret, Essence of Compassion; the buddha called Great Secret, Essence of Joy; the buddha called Great Secret, Essence of Equanimity; the great yoginī called Great Secret, Sovereign Lady of Bliss; the great yoginī called Great Secret, Sovereign Lady Who Crushes; the great yoginī called Great Secret, Sovereign Lady of Joy; the great yoginī called Great Secret, Pervading Sovereign Lady; the retinue of the utterly immeasurable.

And further, the retinue of the great play, namely: the buddha of great play called Yakṣa Heruka; the buddha of great play called Rakṣa Heruka; the buddha of great play called Bhigśa Heruka; the buddha of great play called Viśva Heruka; the yoginī of great play called Yakṣa Śorikā; the yoginī of great play called Rakṣa Śorikā; the yoginī of great play called Bhigśa Śorikā;

and the yoginī of great play called Viśva Śorikā; the retinue of the utterly great play.

And further, the retinue of the great terrifiers, namely: the buddha of great terror called Teśa Heruka; the buddha of great terror called Keśa Heruka; the buddha of great terror called Paśa Heruka; the buddha of great terror called Viśva Heruka; the yoginī of great terror called Teśa Śorikā; the yoginī of great terror called Keśa Śorikā; the yoginī of great terror called Peśva Śorikā; the yoginī of great terror called Viśva Śorikā; and the others, the retinue of the utterly great terrifiers.

And further, the retinue of the great crushers, namely: the buddha of great crushing called Tragśa Heruka; the buddha of great crushing called Hriśa Heruka; the buddha of great crushing called Kaśa Heruka; the buddha of great crushing called Bagśa Heruka; the yoginī of great crushing called Tragśa Śorikā; the yoginī of great crushing called Triśa Śorikā; the yoginī of great crushing called Kaśa Śorikā; the yoginī of great crushing, Bagśa Śorikā; and the others, the retinue of the utterly great crushers.

And further, the retinue of the great devourers, namely: the buddha of great devouring called Ruti Heruka; the buddha of great devouring called Ragti Heruka; the buddha of great devouring called Khahi Heruka; the buddha of great devouring called Saha Heruka; the yoginī of great devouring called Ruti Śorikā; the yoginī of great devouring called Tagti Śorikā; the yoginī of great devouring called Khahi Śorikā; the yoginī of great devouring called Saha Śorikā; and the others, an exceedingly numerous retinue of yoginīs, together with inconceivable herukas, infinite, every one of them.

And further, the retinue of the secret maidens, namely: the secret maiden called Gaurī; the secret maiden called Caurī; the secret maiden called Pramohā; the secret maiden called Vetālī; the secret maiden called Pukkasī; the secret maiden called Caṇḍālī; the secret maiden called Ghasmarī; the secret maiden called Gathering of Mothers; and the others, the inconceivable retinue of the utterly secret maidens.

And further, the retinue of the maidens of joy, namely: the maiden of joy called Siṃhamukhī, the Lion-Faced; the maiden of joy called Gṛdhramukhī, the Vulture-Faced; the maiden of joy called Vyāghramukhī, the Tiger-Faced; the maiden of joy called Śṛgālamukhī, the Fox-Faced; the maiden of joy called Śvānamukhī, the Dog-Faced; the maiden of joy called Charnel-Bird-Faced; the maiden of joy called Kākamukhī, the Crow-Faced; the maiden of joy called Darkness-Faced; and the others, the inconceivable retinue of the utterly joyful maidens.

And further, the retinue of the utterly devoted maidens, namely: the utterly devoted maiden called Horse-Faced; the utterly devoted maiden called Pig-Faced; the utterly devoted maiden called Monkey-Faced; the utterly devoted maiden, She Who Grinds to Dust; and the others, the retinue of the utterly devoted maidens.

And further, the retinue of the sons, the utterly great slayers, namely: the utterly great slayer called Glorious Tiger-Neck; the utterly great slayer called Glorious Yak-Neck; the utterly great slayer called Glorious Deer-Neck; the utterly great slayer called Glorious Leopard-Neck; the utterly great slayer called Glorious Hayagrīva, the Horse-Neck; the utterly great slayer called Glorious Wolf-Neck; the utterly great slayer called Glorious Lion-Neck; the utterly great slayer called Glorious Bear-Neck; the utterly great slayer called Glorious Boar-Neck; the utterly great slayer called Glorious She-Bear-Neck; the utterly great slayer goddess called Vajra Slayer; the utterly great slayer goddess called Vajra Charnel Ground; the utterly great slayer goddess called Vajra Mortar; the utterly great slayer goddess called Vajra Caṇḍālī; the utterly great slayer goddess called Vajra Arrow-Proud; the utterly great slayer goddess called Vajra Sound-Maker; the utterly great slayer goddess called Vajra Devourer; the utterly great slayer goddess called Vajra Guide; the utterly great slayer goddess called Vajra Remover; and the exceedingly numerous retinue of the utterly great slayer goddesses.

Together with these and others, as many as the finest atoms, he was in union. The consort Vajra Samantabhadrī, Sanga Śorikā, too, entwined herself about his body and, gazing face to face, was in union with him.

Then that Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, knowing that all phenomena dawn within the intention of sameness, entered into absorption in the state called non-referential sameness of body. He fully entered the state called non-referential sameness of speech. He fully abided in the state called non-referential sameness of mind, and apprehended no entering, no withdrawing, and no full turning back.

Then, as that Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, rested unmoving in the state of the expanse, the consort Vajra Samantabhadrī sang this song of emptiness and roused him:

By the power of the utter yoga of emptiness beyond existence and nonexistence,
From what is like the sky, come forth to the sacred pledge;
Ocean of wisdom, expanse of complete liberation,
Arise from the nonabiding, non-referential expanse of space!

Having roused him thus, that Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, knowing that all phenomena dawn as the mind of awakening, brought forth his vajra body from the state of emptiness, just as the stainless moon rises in a clear sky. Then he looked upon the consort Sanga Śorikā and smiled, just as a thousand suns send forth their rays of light.

Then from those rays of light, clouds of awakening gathered thickly, and as they let fall a rain of play, there came thunder of every kind of play, lightning of every kind of play, offerings of every kind of play, songs of every kind of play, dances of every kind of play, drums of every kind of play, conches, cymbals and flutes, ornaments of every kind of play; the grace and the flirting of every kind of play, embracing and lying close, union, dissolving and delighting; feasting and tasting through every kind of play, chewing and bliss, rejoicing, savoring and entering; and he brought about entry into the great joy of every kind of play.

Then the consort Samantabhadrī, with eyes half closed in the manner of one bowing low, gazed upon the Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, and with the hands of yogic union cast her whole body to the ground and made supplication with this song of great play:

Deity of the expanse, heart pledge, who has perfected the two great accumulations,
Body of great play, who perfects enjoyment,
Vajrasattva, glorious and fortunate one, you, my lord,
Through the play of skillful means, arise from the expanse of great bliss!

Then that Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, understanding that he was roused by that song, rose from that state of great bliss into a great miracle, and with majestic bearing, with splendor, with the great mudrā of dance, with various expressive gestures, with stretching and bending of his legs, with shifts of his bodily posture, and with changing knits of his brow, he filled the whole of space with the rays of light of his emanations.

Then that Lord of the Supreme Secret pervaded each and every thing, all signs, forms, existence and nonexistence, and abided as their very nature.

Then Vajra Samantabhadrī was filled with wonder, and again, with the hands of the yoginī, cast her whole body to the ground and made supplication with this song of the arising of miracles:

Body that plays through the illusion of wisdom,
You who have gained mastery of great means and wisdom,
Noble lord, source from which many emanations arise,
Sugata, maker of the going in bliss, please arise!

Then that Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, entered absorption in the samādhi called Suchness of Vajra Play, and remained without speaking at all.

Then the consort Vajra Samantabhadrī, and the great samaya Vajrasattva, and the others, the exceedingly numerous retinue, were filled with wonder, and all of them, with one voice, roused him and made supplication with this song of the suchness of vajra play:

How wonderful!
From the supreme wish-fulfilling body, which is like space,
You perform the vajra dance of all-pervading blessing;
Body swelling with the play of massed clouds of splendor,
You display the miracle of unobstructed vajra play.
How wonderful!
From the supreme wish-fulfilling speech, which is like an echo,
You utter the vajra of melodious blessing;
Speech arrayed with an inexhaustible ocean of qualities,
With a lion's roar you teach the ocean of secret mantra.
How wonderful!
From the essence of wisdom, which is like the sun,
You bestow the vajra bliss of wisdom's blessing;
Mind that is the bindu free of elaboration, nonconceptual,
You display the miracle that ascertains liberation itself.
Beings sunk in the mire of unknowing, folly and delusion,
Bound by the ocean of suffering, you free through skillful means;
Those blinded by the darkness of ignorance, you open through the meaning;
With the boat of play you carry them across the ocean of saṃsāra.
Great mind of all the tathāgatas,
Endowed with the supreme essence of the meaning of wisdom,
Tantra of the yoga of the Great Vehicle, the gathering of all vajras:
Please explain it at length to the assembled host!

Crying a la la ho, they bowed down in certainty and made supplication.

Then that Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, knowing that they had made supplication with that song of the suchness of vajra play, looked upon the whole retinue and smiled. As he smiled, from the expanse of his utterly pure mind, he caused suns of awareness to rise, pervading boundless space, with rays of light beyond counting, beyond even beyond counting. From each one of those rays of light he sent forth a host of exceedingly many vajra herukas. And from each one of those, he spontaneously brought forth a hundred thousand buddhas, each with their own retinue and attendants. From these a rain of suns of awareness fell, and it illuminated every expanse of darkness. There arose the thunder of the Dharma of awareness, the rain of the Dharma of awareness, the many songs of the Dharma of awareness, many dances, and the many offerings of the Dharma of awareness, and in boundless space many sounds, back and forth, high and low, interwoven and calm, and the rest, rang out vividly, pervading everywhere. A rain of offerings also fell. Inconceivable marvelous bodies also appeared vividly; marvelous minds too were brilliantly clear. Inconceivable marvelous speech also resounded, crying a la la ho. And the hundred thousand buddhas too, with inconceivable melodious voices, proclaimed as one sound, "How wonderful! The marvelous meaning of the Dharma of play has come forth!" The hundred thousand members of the retinue and the many kinds of disciples were moved to joyful wonder; they trained the skill of awareness and drew forth the greatness of the meaning; struck with amazement and wonder, all of them remained close by.

Then once more that Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, entered absorption in the samādhi called Vajra Ornament That Purifies the Retinue. Having entered that absorption, he spoke these words of vajra mantra:

hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ
The vidyādharas of the great samaya,
Gathered into the vajra assembly,
Take their seats in the rows of the vajra secret
In due order of higher and lower.
One not of the family becomes one outside the family.
This samaya of the fortunate
Shall not fall to the unfortunate.
If one transgresses it, it destroys him.

hūṃ krodha jvala samaya rakṣa jvala samaya yakṣa jvala samaya citta jvala samaya

As soon as he had spoken these words, from the great blazing mass of light of his tongue there came forth, one after another, wrathful ones in blazing garlands of light, rākṣasas in blazing garlands of light, yakṣas in turbulent garlands of light, and devourers in blazing garlands of light. By these, the rudras, māras, rākṣasīs, yakṣas, rākṣasas, Craving to Enter, Without Craving, Īśvara, Bala, monkeys, goats, ludé, bees, ravens, fire-bearers, saṃsāra, water, earth, space, fire, sound, evil intentions and the rest were destroyed and left without a place to remain, and he made it a marvel indeed.

Then those rays of light coiled in the sky, and the many members of the retinue, greatly afraid, made supplication: "How wondrous! O Blessed One, we pray you, bless us!" At this supplication, that Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, let forth the radiance of his face, and those rays of light became the peaceful, coiling light of wisdom, beautiful and blissful. Delighting all, they purified them into fit vessels, and then those lights dissolved back into the place of light.

Then that Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, rose decisively from the expanse of great nonabiding wisdom, and with a sound like the lion's victorious roar he taught, with this sound, the arrangement of the meaning, the secret certainty, the commentary on the tantra, the play of signs, the great scripture of vajra majesty:

a o oṃ samaya adhi guhya puti aho kula ma toṅ byiṅ ta pa dra amogha īśvar samayana guti a la la ho

Saying this, he spoke nothing at all. He did not speak in any way; he did not speak whatsoever. He did not prevent speech; it was not a stopping. In that very not speaking, he abided as the essence of extensive teaching. Abiding thus, he turned his face to the great samaya Vajrasattva and urged him with a smile.

Then the great samaya Vajrasattva, recognizing that he was being urged, rose from his own seat, touched his own emblem to the five places, placed his tongue upon the lotus, made the prostration of casting his body to the ground in the manner of yoga, and, with the gaze of a lion, looked into the face of the Teacher:

Vajra King, Lord of the Assembly, please heed us!
King of Dharma, unmoving, you act in the manner of a victor;
Supreme victor, king, by the power of the King of Dharma,
Please teach at length to the assembled host!

With a la la ho he made supplication, and that Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, in order to bless that vajra command of activity, entered absorption in the samādhi called Vajra Emanation, and declared:

vajra karma cakra satva raṃ

As he said this, a single wheel of crossed vajras descended into the hand of Sattvavajra, and he obtained the empowerment of the activity vajra. He became one of great play, with exceedingly exalted confidence. Having been blessed, he became wholly undistracted and greatly conscientious.

Then that Sattvavajra took that emblem of activity in his hand, and with various poses and dances, singing various songs in vajra words, he turned that wheel of crossed vajras of activity twelve times, and by turning it through two and five counts, all the realms shook: they shook, shook strongly, shook strongly everywhere; they quaked, quaked strongly, quaked strongly everywhere; they rocked, rocked strongly, rocked strongly everywhere; they churned, churned strongly, churned strongly everywhere; they roared, roared strongly, roared strongly everywhere; they resounded, resounded strongly, resounded strongly everywhere. All the realms became nothing but secret maṇḍalas, and all the beings within them became the mahāmudrā. In a manner of fierce majesty, he remained before the Teacher, in stately bearing, on a seat marked with a lion and a vajra.

Then the great majority of the assembled retinue said this to the Blessed One: "Ah, Blessed One, this Sattvavajra, so mighty, so greatly blessed, so vast, so learned, so clear and profound, has been born from the mind of the Blessed One."

When they had said this, the Blessed One declared: "Great assembled host, this Sattvavajra is the grandmother of all awakening minds, the lord of all compassion, the sovereign of all buddhas, the father of all bodhisattvas and vidyādharas, the great forefather of all yogins, the guide of all who pass into nirvāṇa, the great māra even over all māras, the treasury of all dharmas, and has become the great source of all wisdom. And why is that? From the time of sameness without beginning or end, from beyond even the farthest limit, immeasurable and inconceivable, from boundless time, he has done what was to be done and accomplished the deed, and is manifestly and perfectly awakened. Because he is joined with and abides in the nature of all phenomena, he is born from the mind of all the victors and has become the father of all. So it is."

At these words the whole assembled retinue was filled with wonder: "This Blessed One is so wondrous! This Sugata is wondrous!" They bowed separately to the sublime Sattvavajra, made the prostration of casting the body to the ground in the manner of yoga, made offerings of marvelous flowers and many kinds of gaṇa offerings, and said:

So marvelous and wondrous are you,
Victor born from the mind of the buddhas!
How is it that you appear in this realm?
Son of the victors, who has become the father of yoga,
Since you act in such wondrous ways,
Fully turning the wheel of the word,
The sublime and supreme path of accomplishment,
Indicate it in words and make it clear!

With a la la ho they made supplication.

Then the sublime Vajra turned the vajra and, having twirled it, spoke majestically, indicating the meaning in words:

oṃ āṃḥ hūṃ a la la ho
The son of the victors speaks nothing at all;
By not speaking, he teaches at length.
This Dharma that cannot be taught
Has not been taught by the victors.
So too the Teacher, the supreme victor,
Does not wish to give it voice,
Yet intends to teach it at length.
Phenomena that are unborn
Are held, though unborn, to be the essence.
Whatever has become the meaning of the essence,
It is not good to speak it out in words.
"In elaboration itself lies deviation":
So the victors have rightly taught.
Therefore the Vajra King himself
Intends to give no pronouncement.
Whoever draws out the meanings of the intention,
Just like a reflection in a mirror,
The meaning of the intention appears to awareness.
By the meaning of the unborn essence,
Though it is not to be elaborated in words,
For yogins of signs and words,
Meticulous about words and coarse about meaning,
Of weak skill, whose minds follow after words,
In order to guide them and gladden their thoughts,
The miracle of the referential from the nonreferential,
Indicated by words, is this:
The mind of all sugatas and victors,
That very secret, the meaning of the essence,
Sameness, awakening, the Great Vehicle,
Clearly set out and declared with certainty.
Listen, vast assembled hosts!
I am the essence of all the victors
Who abide in the three times.
For this meaning hidden in emptiness,
With the key of words illustrated by examples,
I open the door to the awareness of sameness,
Draw out the hidden meaning and make it clear.
Like a lamp shining amid rice husks,
Cleared away and lifted, its light is found and stays vivid.
Because they delight in profound conduct,
For whoever is familiar with the meaning,
The nature of the conduct of secret mantra
Is set out by ten matching examples,
And the yogin should understand it:
Illusion, mirage and dream,
Reflection, the city of the gandharvas,
Echo, the moon in water, optical illusion,
Bubble, and the false appearance of a firebrand.
Practice, and the conduct of secret mantra,
Accomplishment, and the abodes,
The sound of recitation, and mudrā: consider these;
Samādhi, view and miracle: examine these.

Just as with the character of an illusion,
From the efforts of people
And the application of medicines and mantras,
Whatever appears that captivates the senses,
How could there be any coming or going in it?
So too the character of practice:
From the application of syllables and substances,
Though it appears accomplished, it has no intrinsic nature.
Just as with the character of a mirage,
In the months of the farming season,
From the conjunction of warmth and sunlight,
In the nature of tormented, childish beings,
Whatever perception of water arises,
How could it ever bring their being satisfaction?
So too the distinctions of the names of secret mantra:
Upon emptiness, whose nature is not apprehended,
Conventions of signs are imputed as names;
Since that itself is empty, names are not apprehended.
For example, the nature of a dream:
When a person falls under the power of sleep,
For a moment, an instant, a little while,
Or likewise for a year and more,
One experiences pleasure and pain alike,
Yet on waking finds nothing at all.
So too the conduct of mantra:
In the nature of emptiness,
Whatever forms of conduct appear,
When examined, are free of all reference.
The character of the appearance of a reflection:
For example, from a face and a mirror,
Dependently an image of the face arises,
Yet how could the face have moved across?
So too the accomplishments of mantra:
In dependence on practice, samādhi and implements,
Though accomplishments arise in some manner,
How could there be any object granted or received?
The nature of the gandharvas' dwelling:
On the surface of the sky or the sky of the mind,
Wherever the forms of gandharvas arise,
Their city too arises there;
And having arisen, it too is impermanent.
So too the abodes of accomplishment in secret mantra:
On the surface of the dharma sky or the sky of the mind,
Wherever the yogin generates the Lord of Secrets,
The celestial palace where he dwells
Arises, yet he does not come to dwell in it.
The sign of an echo resounding:
For example, with sound as its condition on a cliff face,
An echo arises just like the sound;
Though it arises, there is no sound heard to apprehend.
So too the sound of reciting the mantra words:
Relying on signs imagined, as it were, as existent from the nonexistent,
It is empty sound,
Taught as mere utterance.
Just as with the moon in water,
The moon rises in an unobscured sky,
And its reflection appears in clear water;
Though it appears, there is no thought of grasping it.
So too the mudrā that appears as form:
Clear as a body from the sky of the mind,
The mudrā appears before the vidyādhara;
Though it appears in this way, it is free of thoughts of grasping.
Just as with a stream of rain,
Bubbles arise from the water,
And from that single form
Many kinds in many numbers arise,
Gather, and break apart again into fragments;
So too, for the yogin's practice,
Samādhi is held to be of many kinds.
The nature of delusive appearance:
From the empty thing free of elaboration,
A life force, a potent one, an agent,
A sentient being and the rest appear,
As things appear like substances to diseased eyes;
It is nothing but delusion appearing.
So too the view of mantra:
However many miracles are displayed,
In the nature without appearance,
It is nothing but various delusions appearing.
Thus does the yogin view it.
Just as with the nature of a firebrand:
Whoever holds a firebrand in hand
And whirls it, it appears as an unbroken wheel,
Yet there is no nature of a wheel in it.
So too the miracle of mantra:
From the nature of the sameness of the unequal,
The meaning arises just as it is focused on,
And various miracles flow unceasing.
Therefore all phenomena with signs
Are one within the state of signlessness;
Whatever habitual tendencies of thought arise,
Since all phenomena are one within the expanse,
All signs of thought, of its movement and elaboration,
Within the unarisen, unborn expanse itself,
Do not stir, and yet miracles arise.
Whoever sees this treasure jewel
From which various qualities abundantly arise,
All the qualities of all the buddhas
Gather in that one in a single instant.
Therefore take up this wish-fulfilling jewel
Without turning back.
Supreme treasure of the genuine ultimate,
Supreme wealth of all yogins:
Whoever realizes it gains it, the one alone;
For others without connection it is hard to obtain.
Selfless, free of duality, expanse of great bliss,
The great bliss of all the buddhas,
Primordially spontaneous and uncompounded,
Bindu, the nature of reality, primordially boundless,
All-pervading and all-spreading, it gathers into the essence:
The nonreferential awakening mind free of elaboration.
This definitive meaning, appearing as the profound,
Is like a knot in space, its depth hard to fathom.
Yogins bound to reference, of small intellect,
Whoever is of weak skill and thinks about it,
Examining it in a wavering way,
Will not realize it, even though they examine.

For example, if with the faculty of the eye
One closely examines the core of the sun,
One does not realize what one examines,
But the eye is dazzled by its rays.
Just so, by the nature of the childish,
However they examine the profound meaning,
They do not see that genuine meaning;
Dull-minded children grow weary and faint.
Phenomena that cannot be examined
Are not realized by those with conceptual thought;
For whoever does not realize that meaning,
How is it any different from falling over a cliff?
Unheard from the very beginning, it is supremely virtuous;
Being free of thought, leave it just as it is.
This dharma left unexamined
I have not taught to the childish.
Even if it were taught, they would not understand it;
It cannot be taught and is hard to understand.
Free of all expression in words,
How could it be pointed out as "this"?
Being nonexistent, the object of teaching cannot be singled out.
Train the mind in the nonreferential dharma,
And when the mirror of the mind is made clear,
If one closely examines pure space,
The nature without appearance
Becomes itself just like space.
The nature of reality is like space,
And space too has no characteristic.
Whatever name gives expression to space,
That expression itself is a thing;
Yet though one utters the name of that thing,
It does not become an obstruction to space.
Likewise, what gives expression to nonconceptuality,
That expression itself is a concept;
Yet though one utters the name of that concept,
It does not become an obstruction to nonconceptuality.
In just that way, look at the mind as well:
Mind itself is not established as something else,
And likewise phenomena are not established either.
Mind and the nature of reality are not two;
Whatever is mind itself is the nature of reality,
And the nature of reality is just the same.
To be endowed with the nonduality of phenomena and mind
Is taught to be the great unsurpassed awakening.
Mind is like space;
Free of concepts, what does it not realize?
Likewise, like the great earth,
It grows the sprout of unsurpassed awakening.
Since it appears like the nature of fire,
It burns up the firewood of delusion.
Since it is the nature of wind,
It clears away all the dust of unknowing.
Since it is the nature of water,
It cools all the torment of the afflictions.
Since it is the nature of medicine,
It draws out the chronic sores of saṃsāra.
Likewise, being the nature of nectar,
It descends as nourishment for the living.
Likewise, being the nature of a sharp blade,
It cuts through the ocean of suffering.
By appearing like the sun,
It clears the dark house and shows the light.
Being by nature like Mount Meru,
It is steadfast, unchanging and unmoving.
Being by nature like the ocean,
Its blessing on the unmoving is great.
Because mind works its miracles in this way,
Saṃsāra and nirvāṇa
Do not appear as two;
From the good and bad nature of the mind
The perception of two is set apart and shown.
From mind arises genuine wisdom;
From it likewise arise happiness and suffering.
From it arise the Dharma and the wisdom of the buddhas,
And so too does the Saṅgha.
From this arise the perfections and the accumulations,
Awakening, and the lotus.
From this arise Vajrasattva,
The stainless Jewel, and the Giver of Courage;
Likewise the Vajra Immeasurable
And the Various Victor arise from this.
From this arise the various mudrās
And all the heart essences of awareness.
From this arise attachment, aversion,
Suffering, pride and jealousy,
And delusion likewise.
From this arise all the three realms,
Men, women and those of neither sex.
From this arise desire and the torment of desire,
The afflictions, torment and attachment.
Mountains and beings likewise,
Space and the winds,
And burning fire fully arise.
From this arise earth and water,
Stone, seed and sprout.
Likewise lightning and stars,
Sun, moon and clouds arise.
Likewise all that acts and all that does not act,
Each and every thing comes to arise.
For example, from the nature of space,
Clouds, mist, fog and vapor,
The moon, the sun and the hosts of stars,
Though they appear, remain just like space.
Likewise, from the sky of the mind,
Whatever miracles of signs arise,
The yogin should see them
As remaining like the space of the nature of reality.
Such is mind itself;
In its nature, realized or not realized,
Views are seen to differ one from another.
To those with conceptual thought,
Thought appears as the very essence of delusion.
To those on the path of the close placements,
It appears entirely as a heap of particles.
To those who practice the accumulations and the close placement of compassion,
Everything is like the nature of illusion.
When one abides knowing it as sameness itself,
One abides seeing the nature of space.
In the space of wisdom's self-appearance,
It simply appears as space itself.
Beyond expression, without seeing, free of reference:
This meaning of non-seeing
Was taught by the victors of the past.
In the awakening of the essence of the sugatas
There is no intention other than this.
While one is endowed with that view,
In the samaya that is like space,
Free of the sides of keeping and not keeping,
There is nothing to keep, saying "this."
However many kinds of keeping
The victors of the three times have taught,
The sūtras on keeping are gathered into one:
The yogin who is like space
Keeps the samaya that resembles space.
And that too is bliss, when there is nothing to keep.
Others, those of the lesser vehicles,
Guard the boundaries of keeping
And adopt and reject among phenomena of fault and virtue.
Phenomena free of keeping,
Since there is nothing to keep, leave just as they are.
In the samaya of suchness,
Rest the mind just as it is;
With mind itself, which never forgets,
Bind yourself within the state of nothing to keep.
This samaya of space, with nothing to keep,
According to the nature in which it is known,
Appears separately as vows and moral discipline.
The childish bind themselves with oaths;
The Teacher is known to teach the two truths in the causal vehicle;
In the unsurpassed supreme vehicle, samaya is practiced.
Though not moving from the single meaning,
It is known separately according to those to be trained.
The supreme samaya is endowed with suchness;
Whoever is endowed with it is sublime,
And whoever transgresses it will be burned.
Therefore the great samaya
Has been taught by the hero to be like space.
For all the victors and heroes
There is nothing whatever beyond this samaya.
As for the distinctions of conduct:
In the nature of reality, free of all doing and seeking,
There is no reference to what is accomplished or to accomplishing.
Beings who strive to cut off delusion
Go wrong by bringing a mind of suffering and striving
Into nonreferential awakening, great bliss.
In the nature of reality, where there is nothing to do,
To practice afflicted effort where there is no doing is delusion.
While the self-arisen, the spontaneously present, abides,
What need is there to seek through childish conduct?
Whoever desires it, what is it they would accomplish?
Whatever one practices, accomplishes or seeks,
It is suffering itself, and there is no awakening.
For hell, the great buddha,
Suffering is the very path of awakening.
The two grounds of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas,
And whatever distinctions of vehicles have been taught,
All that involves doing and striving is delusion.

And is a great obstacle to unsurpassed awakening.
The meaning of great bliss, which is not to be sought:
If it is not seen through this expanse of mind,
Whatever accomplishment and striving one may practice,
The childish wander in futility, to no purpose.
In an intrinsic nature that cannot be sought,
What is there to do by a conduct of striving?
In the nature of reality, which is without grasping,
There is no mistaken striving that clings to words.
In the nature of reality, which cannot be traversed,
The maṇḍala of traversing is free even of a name.
In the nature of reality, which cannot be conceived,
There is no object for the thinking of conceptual mind.
In the nature of reality, which is free of seeking,
The unsought is spontaneously present from the very beginning.
Having no edge and no center, its expanse is great.
Having no outside and no inside, it is primordially clear and all-pervading.
Without faults or virtues, it is spontaneously complete.
Since there is nothing to accomplish, it is free of a seeking mind.
Since there is nothing to grasp, it is free of any object of reference.
Since it knows all, its blessing is great.
From the very beginning it abides in the heart of awakening.
Such is the distinction of conduct.
Therefore, although there is nothing but the one,
In the nature of striving that conforms to conduct,
Conceptual conducts appear as separate.
All delusion is conduct within saṃsāra;
Unfailing cause and effect is conduct in the true meaning;
The nonreferential is conduct in space.
Though one may practice all these conducts in this way,
The ultimate meaning is itself the supreme conduct.
For all who have become sublime,
There is no sublime conduct other than that.
Thus has the supreme being declared.
Know it, free of doubt!

Unfabricated awakening is suchness.
In the nature of reality, which cannot be meditated upon,
Meditation has no object of reference,
And it cannot even be designated "samādhi."
Even if one meditates, it is not found;
Likewise, if one does not meditate, it is not lost.
However various the objects of meditation that appear,
Once they are meditated upon, one does not abide in its state.
Therefore they are the domain of the childish.
For example, children, by their very nature,
Make various shapes out of earth and the like,
Make them beautiful, and look at them for an instant.
Even as they are seen, they are not established;
In a single instant they fall apart
And appear as the dust of earth and the like.
Likewise childish yogins
Meditate on various things such as forms.
In the instants of the mind's particulars,
Various movements of mentation arise.
Even as they arise, they are not established.
When, in mindfulness of the instant of mind,
The objects of mentation are cut off,
One abandons the extreme of cutting off the awareness of what arises,
And focuses on phenomena as being like space,
Inexpressible and uninterrupted.
Unerring with regard to that meaning,
Unforgetting samādhi is the supreme meditation.
Why? Because the very realm of phenomena (dharmadhātu)
Is unconditioned, like space.
Since it cannot be conceived, it is like space.
Since it cannot be expressed, it is free of form.
In the all-ground, which is space throughout,
Mentation is like the sun rising in the sky.
Consciousness is covered by the clouds of affliction,
And, stirred everywhere by the wind of formations,
It brightens and arises, enters, clears and ceases.
Through consciousness's conceptions of signs,
The space of the all-ground is not allowed to be seen.
To see the all-ground is itself the dharmakāya.
Not forgetting the sky that is this meaning
Is the great samādhi, uninterrupted.
Whoever holds this meaning in mind,
However the things of form may appear,
That one's mind has passed into nirvāṇa from the very beginning.
All the buddhas of great bliss
Have no intention whatsoever other than this.
While this space, which is without appearance,
Abides as the great samādhi,
What would one seek apart from it?
Even if the Victors sought it, they would never find it.
Whoever seeks somewhere other than this
Is worn out in the very seeking.
The other, or lesser, doors
Err by bringing a grasping samādhi
Into the nature of reality, which is without samādhi.
Whatever is practiced in them
Is like threading a most fine thread
Through the eye of a needle into space.
What the childish do,
The mighty ought not to do.
The space in which the mighty move
Will not fit within the space of a needle's eye.
Therefore this is not taught to others;
If it were taught, the childish would be undone.
The space-like reality of the nature of reality
Is made clearly manifest by the sun of a thousand rays.
The mere garlands of the light rays of blessing,
Appearing like an illusion, are taken as signs.
The unmoving, signless reality of intrinsic nature,
By those who have not seen it with unborn mind,
Appears as a game that deceives children.

The mind itself, which is the great accomplishment,
Is the jewel from which whatever one wishes arises.
However one focuses on it, it is transformed accordingly,
And all the great aims of accomplishment are fulfilled.
If mind sees the way of mind,
What is called accomplishment comes from that.
Who bestows it, and who receives it?
Neither what is bestowed nor a bestower is found.
For example, water, a mirror,
The sky, a sword, crystal and the like,
Whose nature is to make things clear,
Are clear to look at when undisturbed, and so on;
That is the nature of water and the rest,
And there is nothing very wondrous in it.
In the clear water and the sky of the mind,
If it is not muddied by the clouds of affliction,
The accomplishments spread out like the sun, the moon and the hosts of stars,
And unobscured, blessings dawn naturally.
That all-ground, the essence of space,
Is piled deep with the clouds of affliction.
To want, without scattering them with the wind of wisdom,
To clear away the obscurations and make the nature of reality clear
Through the antics of a jester
Is like children wet with water
Who, when the sun is thoroughly obscured
By the shadow of the clouds,
Blow with the breath of their mouths
At the heaped mass of great clouds,
Wanting to clear the clouds and make the sunlight bright.
Therefore the lord of yogins
Should examine the meaning of the mind itself
And come to know the root, the all-ground.
When one looks at that mind itself, it is clear.
Whatever possesses a root,
Though times and seasons and years go by,
Produces branches, leaves and fruit;
The seed of the root does not become nothing.
Just so, when yogins of application,
With childish minds, wish to take
Accomplishments marked by strenuous effort,
They too are gratified by signs.
For example, the nature of fire,
Which burns the earth and the forests,
From the truth of the coming together of causes,
Appears as the truth of existence arising from nonexistence.
Likewise, the yoga of signs, too,
Wishing to attain the miracles of signs,
From the coming together of the causes of signs
Appears as signs that gratify signs.
For the Victors, who shine exceedingly like stars,
And greatly practice the conduct of awakening,
By focusing intently on the ultimate meaning,
The accomplishment of the supreme secret awareness is swift.
Understanding the great and profound meaning
Through a nature that does not conceive of signs,
One should settle it by the definitive meaning
And have confidence in the meaning of equality.
Just as "space" is spoken of as a mere word,
And by its very nature is free
Of the conventions of expression and of what expresses it;
Just as a shimmering mirage
Is nonexistent even as it appears;
So the nature of all phenomena is clear,
And even in its clarity it is not apprehended.
The great skillful means, the deep path of wisdom,
Free of elaboration, has passed beyond the narrow defile of signs.

Since its reach is exceedingly vast, it is free of two paths.
The path of equality, where neither center nor extreme is apprehended:
Having entered it, as one, into equality,
Free of objects and of a seeking mind,
And having abandoned both extremes of adopting and rejecting,
One abides in the manner of not abiding.
Because all is of one taste in the expanse of the nature of reality,
Knowing and the knowable are not two.
If knowing itself has known phenomena from the very beginning,
What need is there to seek the nature of reality elsewhere?
For example, just as illusory pleasure and pain,
Once cleared away, are neither of them apprehended,
So the changing pleasure and pain of saṃsāra
Are equal, equal in the meaning of their intrinsic nature.
Just as, when all times
Have been realized as equality,
If one does not follow after the words of discussion,
How could the times that have become equal be apprehended?
Just as space, being naturally without form,
Cannot be caught as an object of reference,
So mind cannot fathom the unborn meaning.
Having understood the dividing of faults and virtues into two,
And having abandoned the meaning of two,
If one rests in the meaning of natural equality,
"Meditation" is nothing but a name that is spoken.
Because a dualistic view has never arisen,
Whatever perceptions of duality may occur,
Phenomena, self-arisen, peaceful and unobstructed,
Are clear in the meaning of the primordially unfabricated.
The unborn, utterly pure mind of awakening (bodhicitta)
Is equal to the mind of non-abiding wisdom.
Through the distinction between awareness and unawareness,
Saṃsāra and nirvāṇa appear in dependent relation.
Because there is no conceptual thought, there is nothing to pacify.
It is not grasping, and it is not "free of elaboration."
Except for teaching the provisional meaning,
In truth it cannot be expressed in words.
In a nature that appears yet does not exist,
No mind arises that thinks a knowing intellect exists.
In this meaning of equality and emptiness,
One does not superimpose an emptiness that has a meaning.
For one who possesses the knowing, free of conceptions of signs,
Of the unfabricated, peaceful intrinsic nature,
If one does not apprehend even the mere "it does not exist,"
How could one grasp at the awareness of what arises?
Since everything is non-abiding awakening,
Although it is not accomplished by seeking,
Just as one is free of delusion once an illusion has cleared,
One abides in the heart of wisdom free of elaboration.

To conceive of it as saṃsāra is poison.
If one does not know the suffering of saṃsāra,
How could happiness come from drinking poison?
Well compounded, poison itself becomes medicine.
The actions of saṃsāra appear in just that way.
Like the stabbing pain of poison, deluded appearance hard to bear,
The suffering of saṃsāra, born of ignorance, appears.
When, through the unending magical illusion of a dream,
One is utterly worn out and tormented,
It is like being seized by Māra, the Lord of Death,
And no other protector or holy one can help.
Like an elixir that dispels the māras,
One should realize the perfect, supreme path.
Just as, when one is purged of poison, sickness and madness,
Unable to bear it for even an instant,
One strives with medicines and physicians,
Yet once cured, these are no longer apprehended,
So in delusion, where saṃsāra is not apprehended,
Like striving to cure a sickness in a dream,
Though one strives at accomplishment and samādhi,
To realize saṃsāra as empty is itself awakening.
Just as the cause and effect of an illusion do not fail,
So it appears to the foolish and the deluded.
For those who have the skill of the awareness of equality,
Saṃsāra is a mere word for delusion;
The unerring path of awareness is clear in their minds,
And they have no conceited thought of attaining it by effort.
If one is not equal with the meaning of the mind itself,
Equality in words does not fathom the meaning.
Although deluded saṃsāra has never moved from equality,
Wrong conceptions appear, merely in the manner of an illusion.
Having known the mind itself as primordially awakened,
Diligently applying oneself to the meaning of awareness,
And gathering the power of self-arisen qualities,
How could one be bound up with delusion?
When the intention that possesses the mind of awakening is complete,
There is the body of equality, possessing supreme wisdom;
The empowerment of the master, possessing supreme samādhi;
And, through bodily conduct possessing the supreme accumulation of virtue,
The form body, possessing supreme merit.
One does not look on it as accomplished by way of ripening,
Nor does one obtain it by seeking:
The very time of the cause is the supreme result.
The samādhi of the examining mind itself
Engages the meaning at the very time of being a sentient being.
One does not travel across the spontaneously present ground;
Knowing the ground of saṃsāra, one abides in bliss.
Even the suffering of saṃsāra, in which one does not abide,
Is bound up with awakening, the path through saṃsāra.
The way in which the characteristics of illusion are not blocked
Is self-arisen, free of conception, equal as one.
Firmly in the meaning of equality,
The nature of reality is clear even within the nature of reality.
Although one speaks the words "equal through equality,"
That does not accord with the meaning of equality.
That awakening, too, is not apprehended.
Whatever one speaks of as "non-abiding,"
While grasping at a place to abide and a place of reference:
That is what is called apprehended and apprehender.
If there were no illusion-like mind of awakening,
Then the teaching that the empty, free of things and without objects,
Does not abide intrinsically in space,
Would not exist.

Making the body the chief thing in meditative concentration,
And the places, and all the mudrās of the body,
Arise from fixated clinging to signs.
In the signless mind there is no seeking of things to do.
If the body has become like a mirage,
There is no reference to places or to mudrās.
They are taught for the sake of action and conduct,
Yet there is nothing to do, nor even a reference to doing.
Since suchness is unborn and without a basis,
There is no adjusting the body for a pleasant place.
To practice the path of contrivance out of attachment
Is itself the conceptuality of apprehended and apprehender.
The bodily solitude of meditative concentration is not solitude;
Solitude of mind in space is the supreme solitude.
The mind itself is the place of solitude;
This is the supreme place for the conduct of yoga.
A roofed hut or a mountain cave is not the celestial palace;
The mind itself, boundless, is the supreme celestial palace.
This is the marvelous palace of jewels.
Without outside or inside, it is inside throughout.
Those gathered together in a gathering are not the assembly;
The gathering of wisdom in the mind is the supreme assembly.
Since all phenomena are gathered there, nothing is left out:
It is the assembly that delights countless Victors.
Conventional riches are not the requisites;
The mind itself is the riches, endowed with the substances.
If one knows this supreme and special substance,
There is no doubt that practice will here be accomplished.
To perform meditation is not meditation;
There is nothing to think of as an object of reference.
Meditating thus on the secret of mind,
To meditate on mind in truth is the supreme samādhi.
"Guard," it is said, yet nothing to be guarded is apprehended.
That mind itself, which is hidden in emptiness:
If, having passed beyond signs, one knows the words,
That is the sublime secret to be guarded.

To say "equal" in words is not to apprehend equality.
Beyond the self-purified equality of the mind itself,
There is no further meaning of equality.
To know this meaning as equal is the meaning of equality.
The conduct of ascetic discipline is not the conduct;
The playful conduct of mind settling into mind,
Of great and vast skillful means, is the supreme conduct.
Since, as the source of the meaning of awakening,
It abides primordially in the source of mind,
No name of signs, of petitioning or attaining, is apprehended,
And the great accomplishment is uninterrupted.
When mind thus sees mind,
Wisdom sees the nature of reality.
At all times there is no arising or ceasing.
One comes to see a meaning never seen before,
And yet one sees no thing at all.
All is without intrinsic nature,
Merely flickering in the intellect.
When one sees a meaning such as this,
There is nothing whatever seen as a sign.
Knowing its way to be a mere illusion,
The meaning equal to space appears.
Some have doubts about that meaning:
"If nothing at all is seen,
Why is it designated 'appearing'?
That contradicts the meaning of nonexistence."
To them: since that very meaning, which is not seen,
Appears before mentation,
For that reason it should be called "appearing."
Therefore, so long as delusion is not exhausted,
That space which is not seen,
Though it appears to mentation,
Does not appear as form.
If the mind itself is not truly seen,
The signless meaning clear to mentation
And the deluded movement of signs, these two,
Move intermingled and do not come apart.
If that very thing comes to be truly seen,
Then, in the very next instant,
The movement of mentation is cut off,
One is free of the former deluded phenomena of signs,
And by that the truth is seen.

Therefore, great gathered assembly,
The mind is itself the perfect path.
Like the trackless path of a bird in the sky,
The Guide has called it the path without deviation.
That path explained in this way,
So that everyone may realize it,
Is taught by indicating it in words,
Yet it is nonreferential and beyond the domain of words.
Since that is its nature,
Moving from the ground of the intrinsic nature into signs,
And because names are applied to what has no name,
Doubts arise as to what is and what is not.
From that it becomes a basis for dispute,
And to dispel this, through the many categories
Of the collections of words of signs,
Countless doors have been taught.
Through these the definitive meaning is traced,
And the meaning is sought by the lamp of words
Brought forth from the expanse of the enlightened mind.
If one asks what the path itself is like:
If one follows after arising thoughts,
One circles in the abode of saṃsāra.
Whatever blocks the movement of arising thoughts
Becomes mere cessation.
To be dull, without any arising thoughts,
Is to become the dark path of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas).
If one holds to a single "this" as mind,
It is a mountain of conceptions of signs.
If one cuts it off, saying, "There is no before and after,"
That is "nonexistence," the extreme view of annihilation.
Whatever arising and ceasing occurs,
Moving even for an instant from the meaning of the ground,
Increases the path of enumerations;
How could it be the nature of reality of the path?
For example, wherever they go, children
Seek to be pure by getting rid of their shadow;
But since it arises from themselves by its very nature,
If they search, they do not find themselves shadowless.
While the meaning itself abides from the very beginning,
To seek that meaning to the point of exhaustion
With the mind's various sufferings
Is pointless, the work of Māra.
To express the meaning in words
Is like arguing about space.
Though medicine and treatment are explained in words,
That does not help the crowd of the sick.
If one does not know the mind itself in truth,
Though one expresses the meaning in words, one does not realize the meaning.
To abandon saṃsāra and seek nirvāṇa
Is like discarding the body and looking for its shadow.
If one asks what the path itself is like:
Ignorance and wisdom are not different;
Non-realization and realization are one as the path.
Since this mind appears as manifold,
The manifold is one as mind.
For example, from what is one as gold,
Shapes and forms that differ
Are known by children under separate names;
Though so known, they are one in the essence of gold.
Likewise, in the nature of mind,
Through the distinction of deluded and undeluded,
Though everything appears there however one looks,
Even as it appears, nothing other than mind is apprehended.
A jewel blazing like a lamp,
Even if it is hidden in a foul pit,
Since its very qualities shine by themselves,
Its light becomes clear in the sky.
Likewise, the mind itself, the jewel lamp,
Though body and speech have sunk into saṃsāra,
Since it is self-arisen and self-pacified and shines brilliantly,
Wisdom is clear in the sky of the nature of reality.
The mind itself is just like space:
All-pervading, empty and clear, from it the manifold arises.
All awareness of what arises, all movement and stopping,
Arise by their nature and subside of themselves.
In brief, since it changes just as one examines and thinks,
Everything appears as an entity.
In the unfabricated, which is itself without movement,
The manifold movements and arisings are not faults,
And the miraculous display is unceasing and without fear.
It is simply resting the mind, undistracted,
In the nature of space, just as it is.
That is the great purpose of the supreme being.
That mind is the essence
From which the buddhas of all three times arise.

When, for a long time, one has come
To know and be familiar with such a meaning,
The experiential warmth of seeing the definitive meaning,
The great signs of meeting the meaning,
Dawn as eight, according to the scripture of the pith instructions,
And one will certainly experience three meanings.
These are taught as the signs of the great warmth
Of having arrived at the point.
The warmth that arises incidentally from that:
The mind of awakening, of compassion
For the phenomena of saṃsāra, will arise.
A perception of distrust arises
Toward phenomena conceived as signs.
A perception of wonder and inquiry arises:
"Phenomena being like this,
How amazing it is to be deluded like this!"
Such a perception will arise as this:
"Beings who do not know this meaning,
Through great means of difficult striving and accomplishment,
Seek the true meaning on so long a path;
Though there will be a result of what they accumulate,
They will never be able to see that meaning."
The experiential warmth of knowing in this way:
For any person who has the good fortune,
Without the progression of the tantra being explained in words,
Hundreds of thousands of teachings arise from mind,
And everything is clear at the doors of speech.
One is able to bring forth, indicating them in words,
Reasons that are certain about the meaning.
That is inseparable and nondual
With the dharmakāya, equality.
It is not comparable
To the supports, the images of body, speech and mind.
It is not comparable
To the two faults, a quiescent body and final cessation.
The Victor has said it is not comparable either
To the heroic beings training on the grounds.
Vidyādharas and sages who examine the meaning
Are alike in body, but in mind are not at all alike.
Therefore, since the great pure path
Is this secret of mind,
Fortunate great gathered assembly,
Apart from knowing this path
There is no reason to seek anything.
By knowing this very path,
Without any difficult striving and accomplishment,
All, all things will be accomplished.

Therefore, great gathered assembly, this mind is called "the path." It is called "saṃsāra." It is called "sentient being." It is called "a boat."

It is called "the journey." It is called "awakening." It is called "mind." It is called "wisdom." It is called "samādhi." It is called "the abode." It is called "the excellent." It is called "the supreme." It is called "the foremost." It is called "the unsurpassed." It is called "the profound." It is called "the secret." It is called "the hard to fathom." It is called "the Dharma." It is called "bodhisattva." It is called "buddha."

Why is that? Because it enters, it is the path. Because it is deluded, it is saṃsāra. Because it does not know, it is a sentient being. Because it carries across, it is a boat. Because it moves on, it is the journey. Because it is blissful, it is awakening. Because its mindfulness is certain, it is mind. Because it is skilled, it is wisdom. Because it is clear, it is samādhi. Because it has arrived, it is the abode. Because it is without equal, it is the excellent. Because it is wondrous, it is the supreme. Because it is noble, it is the foremost. Because nothing has power over it, it is the unsurpassed. Because it is deep, it is the profound. Because it is concealed, it is the secret. Because it is subtle, it is the hard to fathom. Because everything arises from it, it is the Dharma. Because it treads the grounds, it is a bodhisattva. Because it has reached the end, it is a buddha.

Great being, for that reason this path of yoga should be known. This path is the ground of all phenomena. It is the mother of all the buddhas. Why is that? Because all phenomena arise from it, it is the ground. The buddhas who have reached the end have gone by this path. The Teachers of the present, too, go by this path. Therefore this path should be known.

That being so, for that reason this path is also called the father of all the tathāgatas. Likewise it is also called the mother, the son, the lord, the lineage, the life, the wealth, the servant, the body, the speech, the mind, the qualities, and the great activity. Why is that? Because of great skillful means, great wisdom, great awakening, great power, suchness, being hard to pass beyond, being inexhaustible in the nature of reality, great compassion, majesty, displaying itself in manifold ways, knowing everything, arising as anything whatever, and manifold deeds, these should be known to be so. For that reason, too, this path should be fully known.

Likewise it is also called great desire. It is called great livelihood, great enjoyment, great requisites, cause, condition, result, conduct, miracle, mudrā, approach, close approach, accomplishment, great accomplishment, great assembly, and total perfection. Why is that? It should be fully known as such because of manifold desires for objects, living by means of reference, acting in a disorderly way, accomplishing what is needed, producing results, being totally inverted, accomplishing aims, accumulating to the point of ripening, becoming the intrinsic nature, doing what is to be done, arising in manifold ways, being shown as signs, bowing down, acting thoroughly, acting on desire, desiring still more, being totally rich, and being spontaneously present.

Furthermore, everything is present in this great gathered assembly: all phenomena should be known to be included in three kinds of yoga. What are the three? The yoga of the cause, the yoga of the condition, and the yoga of the result: these three, too, should be known to be this very thing and to arise from it. How so? As for the cause, those who wish to know and master the one delusion should also know this very path. Likewise, those who wish to know and master the two signs, the three conceptions, the four inversions, the five afflictions, the five aggregates, the five faculties, the six causes, the twelve conditions, the twelve births, the eighteen natures, the eleven conducts, the four ripenings, the five paths, the six abodes, and the three extremes should also know this very path.

Why is that? Because it is the ground of the desired result. Because of not knowing that very path, it is called delusion. Because it has become dual with its objects, it is called sign. Because it conceives of the three times as things, it is called conception. Because it has turned away from certainty, deviating from the meaning, deviating from equality, deviating from the right direction, and deviating from the ground, it is called inversion. Because cause and effect are thoroughly afflicted, being shrouded by darkness, tormented by craving, weighed down as by a tree trunk, hardened by being unbreakable, and thoroughly agitated, they are called afflictions. Because they are thoroughly assembled, being causes assembled, felt as conditions, known as signs, acting in every way, and cognizing, they are called aggregates. Because they are clear at the doors and have power over objects, having power over seeing, power over hearing, power over sensing, power over tasting, and power over touching, they are called faculties. Because they produce results, having power to grasp, making soft, making attached, blowing into motion, being clear in producing, and being obtained in their time, they are called causes. Because they are related as conditions, being seed, root, sprout, shoot, branch, trunk, leaf, flower, fruit, kernel, and drying out, they are called conditions. Because signs arise, the arising of seeing, of appearance, of hearing, of echoing sound, of sensing, of odor, of tasting, of flavor, of touch, of cognition, and of phenomena, it is called birth. Because they possess the nature of signs, holding the nature of seeing, the phenomenon of appearing, the phenomenon of vividness, the phenomenon of hearing, the phenomenon of sound, the phenomenon of clarity, the phenomenon of sensing, the phenomenon of odor, the phenomenon of satisfaction, the phenomenon of tasting, the phenomenon of flavor, the phenomenon of relish, the phenomenon of touching, the phenomenon of the tangible, the phenomenon of certainty, the phenomenon of knowing, the phenomenon of phenomena, and the nature of realization, it is called the nature of reality.

Because they act upon signs, as the conduct of altering perception, the conduct of acting under another's power, the conduct of wavering, the conduct of imputing through discourse, the conduct of departing from one's counterpart, the conduct of burning all around, the conduct of seeking to deceive, the conduct of holding on to attachment, the conduct that is wholly Māra's, perverted conduct, and conduct in dependence on duality, they are called conducts. Because they ripen as results, as complete ripening, gradual ripening, sudden ripening, and ripening through transformation, they are called ripenings. Because they are traversed, as the path of torment, the path of cessation, the path of darkness, the path of the swamp, and because of lightness, they are called paths. Because they are relied upon, as the abode of Endurance, the abode of the Mindful, the abode of the Equal, the abode of Desire, the abode of Strife, and dwelling in Tuṣita, they are called abodes. Because they hold to the extreme of happiness, the extreme of essential nature, and the extreme of a creator and Īśvara, they are called extremes.

Great gathered assembly, the yoga of the condition is as follows. Those who wish to know and master the one knowing should also know this path. Likewise, those who wish to know and master the two meanings, the three modes, the four means, the five dharmas, the six awarenesses, the twelve great deeds, the twelve great miracles, the eighteen wisdoms, the eleven distinctions of the grounds, the four bodies, the five paths, the six great results, and the one great awakening should also know this very path.

Why is that? Because it acts merely as its own condition and becomes the condition for the result. Because that very path is known without error, it is to be known as the one knowing. Because it appears as the two truths, it is called the two meanings. Because it is applied through the three characteristics, as the mode of existing in the concordant case, which is the imputed, the mode of not existing in the discordant case, which is the dependent, and inference, which is neither, they are called modes. Because it is held by the wise, as the means of entering gradually, the means of applying oneself to conditions, the means of entering directly, and because all is completed, they are called means. Because it abides in its intrinsic nature, abiding in the dharma of the cause, the dharma of the path, the dharma of realization, and the dharma of the result, they are called dharmas. Because it knows what will arise later, as mindfulness of the cause, mindfulness of the result, mindfulness of sound, mindfulness of application, mindfulness of place, and mindfulness of direct perception, and because it is aware, they are called the six awarenesses. Because it acts for the sake of others and engages in the welfare of beings, as the mode of the essence, the mode of birth, the mode of play, the mode of great dominion, the mode of becoming free, the mode of great austerity, awakening and taming Māra and turning the wheel, the mode of taming Māra, the mode of displaying miracles, and the mode of passing into nirvāṇa, they are called great deeds. Because it emanates in manifold ways, as the miracle of samādhi, the miracle of appearance, the miracle of magical power, the miracle of universal expression, the miracle of subsequent teaching, the miracle of meditative concentration, the miracle of blessing, the miracle of birth, the miracle of strength, the miracle of qualities, and the miracle of the result, for that reason they are called miracles. Because it knows all outer and inner phenomena, as the knowing of all inner phenomena, the wisdom that knows all outer phenomena, the wisdom that knows all outer and inner phenomena, the wisdom that knows all empty phenomena, the wisdom that knows the ultimate nature of reality, the wisdom that knows all conditioned phenomena, the wisdom that knows all phenomena whose extremes are not apprehended, the wisdom that knows all phenomena of the three times, the wisdom that knows all phenomena in which adopting and rejecting are not apprehended, the wisdom that knows all phenomena of the intrinsic nature, the wisdom that knows all phenomena that are property-bearers, the wisdom that knows all phenomena that hold characteristics, the wisdom that knows all phenomena without reference, the wisdom that knows all phenomena not conceived as signs, the wisdom that knows all phenomena of essential nature, and the wisdom that knows all phenomena in which things are not apprehended. Because they are relied upon as abodes, relying on the awareness that is unstained, the awareness that does not decline, the awareness that is unobscured, the awareness that is complete, the awareness that is unmoved, the awareness of equality, the awareness of nonduality, the awareness of utter purity, the awareness that is wholly excellent, the awareness that is immediate, and the awareness of complete mastery, they are called grounds. Because it is expressed in every way and abides as essential nature, as the essential nature, the nature of reality, complete enjoyment, and emanation as a guide, they are called bodies. Because it is traversed and entered, as accumulation, application, seeing, meditation, and having gone to the exhaustion of the end, they are called paths. Because the cause is meaningful, as being complete as one, knowing everything, being all-pervading, arising as an assembly, having power over everything, and fulfilling every need, they are called results. Because it is fully perfected, it is called the great awakening.

Great gathered assembly, the yoga of the result is as follows. Those who wish to know and master the one unerring should also know this path. Likewise, those who wish to know and master the two certainties, the three qualities, the four intentions, the five greats, the six assemblages, the twelve modes of arising, the twelve samādhis, the eighteen great wisdoms, the eleven great yogas, the four great accomplishments, the five applications, the six great siddhis, and the one Great Perfection should also know this path.

Why is that? Because the cause itself is accomplished as the result, it is settled, and so it is called the unerring. Because there is no doubt, as certainty through skillful means and certainty through wisdom, they are called certainties. Because desires arise, as all-pervasiveness, qualities vast as waves, and being beyond reckoning, they are called qualities.

Because the intention is indirect, through skillful means, there is the intention directed at sameness; because of the characteristics, the intention directed at another time; because of the antidote, the intention directed at another enumeration; and because of transformation, the intention directed at another meaning. Since they intend in these ways, they are called intentions.

Because it is especially exalted, and because it is manifest, the great self, that very thing, great in every way, and complete in every respect, it is called great.

Because it is complete in every respect, being great sameness, great wealth, great gathering, great awareness, great empowerment, and great blending, it is called an assembly.

Because they arise for the sake of others and accord with the ways of beings: moving to the abode, becoming equal, going in beauty, engaging in delight, joining in secret, destroying signs, being without activity, the sound of the lion's voice, definitive perfection, performing the emanation of great majesty, and, because of complete purity, they are called the great modes of arising.

Because it is clear and does not let go, there is the activity of mind, namely: the samādhi of the pure expanse, the samādhi of vivid, shimmering appearance, the samādhi of the inexhaustible treasury, the samādhi of manifest realization, the samādhi of total perfection, the samādhi of the emanation of the great elements, the samādhi of the means of mastery, the samādhi of one-pointed radiance, the samādhi of the perfection of the great accumulation, the samādhi of the jewel sprout, the samādhi of the medicine that overcomes poison, and the samādhi of accomplishing activities. Because of these, they are called samādhis.

Because they are clear as the wisdom of emptiness, the wisdom of the cause, the wisdom from which all arises, the wisdom of letters, the wisdom of mudrā, the wisdom of emanation, the wisdom that does whatever is needed, the mirror-like wisdom, the space-like wisdom, the illusion-like wisdom, the jewel-like wisdom, the sun-like wisdom, the wisdom of appearance, emptiness and clarity, the wisdom of sameness, the discerning wisdom, the wisdom of diligent activity, and the wisdom of the expanse of phenomena, they are called the great wisdoms.

Because one abides in the yoga of aspiration and intention, the yoga of attending on command and of action, the yoga of distinguishing the great families, the yoga of signs, the yoga of the secret door of scripture, the yoga of continuous conduct, the yoga of marks and signs, the yoga of obtaining the great scriptural transmission, the yoga of holding awareness, the yoga of training great skill, the yoga of complete perfection, and the yoga of spontaneous presence, it is called yoga.

Because of accomplishment in sameness, accomplishment in great bliss, accomplishment in complete perfection, and the spontaneous accomplishment of everything, it is called the great accomplishment.

Through joining cause and result, joining skillful means and wisdom, joining the expanse and primordial wisdom, joining mind and phenomena, and joining action and compassion, it is called the great application.

Because the ocean of existence is accomplished as satisfied in the expanse, the ocean of saṃsāra comes to be accomplished as one's own, the great self comes to be spontaneously accomplished, whatever is visualized comes to be accomplished just so, miracles and magical feats are accomplished without obstruction, and consciousness is accomplished as expanding through the essence of the ultimate, they are called the great siddhis.

Because all categories are accomplished as complete in the awakening mind, great bliss, it is called the one Great Perfection.

Moreover, those in the gathering of the great assembly who wish to tread the eleven great distinctive grounds should also train in this path. What are they? The ground where the power of change is not yet settled, the ground of the supporting basis, the ground of crucial application, the ground of irreversible abiding, the ground of continuous training, the ground of miraculous emanation, the ground of advancing distinctively through reliance, the ground where the result is born from the focus, the ground that does not change through abiding, the ground of the nature of reality spread out, and the ground of perfection that masters all. Those who wish to tread these grounds should also train in this path.

Moreover, those in this gathering of the great assembly who wish to master the ten extremes of yogic training should also train in this path. What are they?
