And likewise with the lineage.
The five lineages of the deities of the families
Are set out in their respective families,
And in the homa pit of the vast swirling expanse,
With the blazing nectar of the sacred substances,
It is fitting to burn them, for purification and attainment.
The substances burned are phenomena of birth.
All phenomena that appear to be born
Here appear as if they were real things.
Phenomena, from conditions and from arising,
And miraculous display, from nature itself,
Are held to be birth itself.
These are phenomena of birth;
The self-arisen substances burned dissolve into their own peace.
The sacred substances of the five afflictions
Are offered into the expanse of the five wisdoms.
The sacred substances of the five aggregates
Dissolve into the bodies of the five fathers, the supreme means.
The substances burned that are gathered by the five elements
Are offered into the expanse of the five mothers, wisdom.
The substances burned of the eight consciousnesses
Are the eight mothers of the places of wisdom.

The eight kinds of distraction by objects
Are the eight divisions of the nonreferential expanse.
The substances burned, the karma of the four modes of birth,
Dissolve into the expanse of the four doors of liberation.
The supreme means and the rest, the ocean-like host,
All of their individual qualities
Are proclaimed with vajra songs, and then
One undertakes the offerings that please them.
When all the offerings are complete,
The great matters of whether obscurations are purified or not,
And the matters of whether accomplishment is gained or not,
The learned one should examine in the fire.
Two sounds of the fire and two colors,
Two ways of blazing and six shapes:
These should be thoroughly known.
If the signs are unpleasant,
Since that is an omen of ill fortune,
The great burning purification should be performed extensively.
Then, if the desired signs appear,
Of the accomplishments desired in the fire,
Take whichever of the three kinds has been accomplished.
Having purified by burning the substances of the corpse,
And having applied mantras to the corpse,
With a variety of very fragrant scents
One should anoint and wash the corpse.
With the fluid of the great melted nectar
Smeared on cloth, the corpse is wrapped,
And on whatever places of the corpse there are,
Having arranged all the applications of mantra,
One should seal them with the signs of the mudrās.
Of whichever maṇḍala of the Victors
Is the basis of the rite, its mudrās,
However many they are, equal to that
Should the number of stakes be made.
On each stake, the root heart mantras
Of the deities, without error,
Are written on large cloths and hung.
When the preparation of the corpse is complete,
On all the outer coverings of the corpse
Set out all the habitual tendencies of saṃsāra;
On cloth, birch bark, or paper
Set down all the seeds of saṃsāra.
The five afflictions together with the three poisons,
And the six lineages on the path of birth,
Made into names or into forms,
Should be placed each at its own place.
The elements, the sense sources, the five aggregates,
Along with apprehended and apprehender, as names,
Written in the manner of symbols,
Should be placed specially on the corpses.
Then that body of the corpse
Upon a support, a board, or a platform
The learned one should place.
To undertake the rite of the hearth:
On a maṇḍala base about an arm span around,
Of whichever maṇḍala it may be,
The palaces where the mudrās reside
Should each be marked out with lines.
On top of it, wood is stacked
In a square shape, filled in,
And the four corners are made firm with four pillars.
Three-storied, with an upper floor and a middle chamber,
Its outer edges should be closed in with wood.
On the board of the middle chamber
The corpse's face is turned toward the east.
In the chamber above the corpse,
Drawn on cloth or on paper,
Clearly set out the maṇḍala of the mudrās.
Open four windows in the corpse chamber,
Tie on four dhāraṇī cords of compassion,
And present them to four masters in the four directions.
When the rite of the wood is complete,
On the platform at the outer edge of the wood
Set out the mudrā deities in their order.
When the platform has been completed,
The vajra duties are assigned:
To the master, the Vajra King,
Entrust the office of burning saṃsāra;
With samādhi and wisdom, single-pointed,
He should continually gather and radiate the fire.
To the vajra action master, the Karma,
Entrust the office of completing the activities;
He should complete the sequence of activities.
A great vajra master,
Taking up the full extent of the corpse's misdeeds,
Enumerates and confesses the accumulations of karma.
The vajra master who links the stages
Strings together the nectar offerings for each stage.
With vajra dances and words of song,
The song and dance offerings for each stage,
Led by music, should be presented.
The aspiration of the vajra abode
Is made at the end of each stage and each activity.
The keeper of the vajra-action tallies
Keeps count with the hundred and eight activities
And offers the white tablets into the master's hands.
The keeper of the tallies of the corpse's evil karma,
Having counted the countless,
Burns the tablets of black signs
In the expanse of the fire of wisdom.
Perform the rite of joining with fire.
As for who the deity of fire is:
The buddha of fire is Amitābha;
The goddess of fire is Pāṇḍaravāsinī (the White-Robed One),
The supreme light of all the Victors.
She holds mastery over the element of fire
And is the lord of the warmth of all things.
She has pledged to burn the three realms of existence;
From the activity of burning saṃsāra
She never departs, by the power of her sacred vow.
All the rites of fire
Are entrusted to the Great Mother, the White One herself.
Then, when all the rites are complete,
Whether the corpse's obscurations are purified or not,
Whether the accomplishment of fire is gained or not,
The learned one examines in the fire, as before.
When unpleasant signs occur,
The rite of burning purification should be performed extensively.
If the signs are pleasing, accomplishment is gained.
The great ashes of the corpse
Are ground, and accomplished as the nectar of ashes.
Through that, great merit
Beyond measure will be obtained.

Thus he spoke. From the great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, this is the twenty-sixth chapter, on accomplishing the vajra fire rite.

Then the sublime Sattvavajra spoke these words of vajra mantra, called Blessing All Maṇḍalas.

When he had spoken this, in what is called the rite of the blazing of activity, everything blazed intensely and spread far and wide.

From the great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, this is the twenty-seventh chapter, on the vajra secret mantra.

Then, again, Sattvavajra spoke at length this chapter on the mudrās of movement.

On the two hands of the supreme yogin,
As the blazing liṅga of sun and moon,
Visualize clearly the two, A and the lotus,
And join means and wisdom without duality.
Abiding in the manner of evenness,
Place A itself above the fire:
This is the seal free of all activity.
The ten seeds of A and MA
Radiate and gather, and are turned in every way,
Coiling to the right and embracing to the left:
This is the seal from which miracles arise.
The ten seeds of A and MA,
Each held separately, are made into sprouts,
Raised up high and turned in every way:
This is the cause of becoming the essence.
With MA, in the manner of giving generosity,
And with A, protecting all:
This is the great immeasurable;
It is the mudrā of equanimity toward all.
MA itself is the vessel that snatches blood;
A itself is formed into rows of teeth and fangs:
This is the mudrā that displays
The terrifying manner, the play of all.
Joining the two, A and MA, at the base,
In the manner of rows of teeth and fangs,
In the manner of a mouth gaping wide:
This is the seal of the great Bhairava.
Seizing with MA and pulling,
Rearing up with A and crushing:
This is the mudrā of the great destroyer.
With MA and A, from head and tail,
Making as if to seize,
Drawing and pulling with the mouth:
This is the mudrā of the great devourer.
From the wisdom of A and MA,
Moving them in the manner of right and left,
Apply the means just as each one is;
As the principle of place and object,
The movements are taught as the mudrās of dance.
The hero rises from the seat,
Gazes toward all the directions and intermediate directions,
And bears himself with great poise and majesty.
The seeds of A and MA,
Each struck, are set upon the hips.
Moving in a stately manner,
Circle all around with the stride of the tiger.
Then with the stride of the heavy bird
Circle as before.
Likewise with the strides of tortoise, leopard and tiger,
Of wolf, lion and bear,
Of great boar and brown bear,
Circle with each and every one.
Likewise, having completed the strides
In the gaits of all the birds,
All the maṇḍalas become complete.

Thus he spoke. From the great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, this is the twenty-eighth chapter, on the mudrās of movement.

Then Sattvavajra spoke this chapter on the secret sublime offering.

hūṃ
The great element, space,
Is the treasury of the five great elements.
From space arise the things desired;
May offerings of the things desired arise!
The supreme essences, the full moon and the rest,
The fivefold substances, maṃsa and the rest,
Flowers and the rest, and garlands of sandalwood:
May a variety of offerings arise!
The supreme touch of women,
The great dhāraṇī women who embrace and caress,
The delightful offering of the joining of the organs:
May you be satisfied by the offering of bliss!
Through vajra play, may this supreme feast
Spread everywhere as clouds of offerings!
This mountain heaped up from the three poisons
Is offered into the expanse of body, speech and mind.
This supreme feast, the play of the five poisons,
Dissolves into the five wisdoms.
This sign, the conceptuality of apprehended and apprehender,
Dissolves into the expanse of space and wisdom.
This saṃsāra, made of the phenomena of signs,
Dissolves into the state of the expanse of dharmatā.
This limb, the gathering of the aggregates,
Dissolves into the five aggregates of liberation.
This sign, the great elements as cause,
Dissolves into the five blazing expanses of space.
In this offering and gift of great play
The ocean-like hosts of Victors take their delight.
This unsurpassed offering of the feast
Is the path the Victors traveled in the past,
The means of slaying the three poisons from their depths:
Accept it in keeping with your former vows!
The deathless nectar, the water of the eternal swastika,
Rouses the elements of the corpse;
This that crushes the heap of poisons,
Accept as the offering of medicine, the supreme elixir!

When he had said this, the hosts of offerings arose in billowing abundance, and the ocean-like hosts of Victors were pleased by that enjoyment.

From the great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, this is the twenty-ninth chapter, on the secret sublime offering.

Then Sattvavajra spoke this chapter on vajra song.

Here, the vajra yogins,
Having received the empowerment that appears as approach,
Send forth the eight syllables of meditative melody.
Syllables are the ornament of song;
From the sixteen syllables of song,
Transforming and combining the syllables as its limbs,
The varieties of song are beyond conception.
First, when the meaning of song arose,
With oṃ the songs are led forth.
oṃ too is a miraculous display of a;
a is the syllable of Dharma.
From a arises oṃ, the mind of the Victors;
The Victors arise from the dharmadhātu.
From a arises aṃ, the supreme of speech;
From speech arise the limbs of song,
Renowned as the sixty limbs of melody.
The qualities of song are beyond conception.
The Victors of the past accomplished through song;
The vajra vidyādharas are drawn forth by song;
The lords of yoga achieve accomplishment through song;
The ocean of the teaching is spread through song;
The daughters of existence are attended through song;
Māras and rākṣasas are tamed through song;
An ocean of siddhis is called down through song.
From the center of the expanse of dharmatā
The nine suns of the śrīvatsa have risen,
And though the four doors of liberation are utterly clear,
They are obscured by the dense black darkness of ignorance:
Open the door of awareness!
In the deep pit of saṃsāra's black darkness
The moon of bodhicitta has risen,
So the darkness is dispelled, and the four doors of liberation appear.
The suns of blazing jewels:
As light rises from the heart of the Great Glorious One,
Through the openings in the clouds of habitual tendencies
Its rays fall upon the yogin's heart;
The darkness is dispelled, the door of awareness is opened,
And one should enter into the meaning of yoga.
From the maṇḍala of the heart of awareness,
As the light rays of rulu open the door,
Various miracles arise through emanation,
Making the distinctions of the three realms of existence,
Drying up the ocean of saṃsāra from its depths,
Freeing the six causes of affliction from their places,
Cutting the root of the three poisons at its source,
Taming malicious māras and rākṣasas,
And raining down blessings and siddhis like rain.
Just as, when the doors of the senses open,
The radiance of a smile clearly appears,
So, as the door of the heart of the śrīvatsa opens,
The syllables, the light rays of rulu, arise.
Dark blue, dark yellow, dark red, green,
And dark purple blazing syllables
Are drawn out as garlands, family by family of syllables.
With the drawn-out tone one urges them on, and with the white tone one brings them down.
When the tenkö comes near,
Make the jimgö clearly manifest.
In the birkwa, which is without duality,
With the drenkö draw out the garland of syllables.
In that itself there are six songs:
Shatapa and Gartapa,
Changkadeu and Bangkadeu,
Drengngewa and Dungngewa.
With three hundred limbs of song
They become the supreme delight of the Victors.
Through these six kings of song
The six aims are perfectly fulfilled.
By singing the song Shatapa,
All the malicious ones lose heart.
By singing the song Gartapa,
All who do harm are ground to dust.
By singing the song Changkadeu,
The blazing maṇḍala is offered like fire.
By singing the song Bangkadeu,
The violent and fierce are made to settle down.
By singing the song Drengngewa,
All the miracles of emanation are spread forth.
By singing the song Dungngewa,
The daughters of existence are brought under control.
In the nature of the nine syllables
All the maṇḍalas of the mudrās are complete;
Likewise the wheel with its rim,
And the courtyards and the gateways around them, are clear.
Whatever exists, all that is seen, appears and is heard,
Is all complete as the words of mantra.
The syllables are linked with all the Victors;
They are close to the ground of the wheel.

Through the syllables, all that is aimed at is accomplished,
The great aim, free of attachment and spontaneously present.
Even the distinctions of the four kinds of activity
Become meaningful without any action.
Through the radiating and gathering of the secret syllables
All the Victors and heroes are pleased.
Though all of that is emanated by the syllables,
Its nature is the dharmadhātu, without substance.
From the miraculous display of the blazing syllables
Various sublime wonders arise.
Likewise they burn the firewood of the obscurations;
Wisdom blazes, dispelling the darkness of delusion;
The sun of awareness shines down clearly.
Oneself is the blazing heart of the Great Glorious One:
The citta, an eight-cornered śrīvatsa, blazes,
And the eight jewel syllables are clear.
From the blazing jewel heart of the Great Glorious One,
From the eight-cornered purple śrīvatsa,
Nine golden suns have risen.
The light rays, the eight syllables of rulu,
In a blazing vajra box of wind,
Intensely blazing, radiating and churning,
Are emanated, and through the right nostril
Of the lord of yoga they should be drawn out.
In a palace of gorocana
The size of a rounded fingernail,
Nine golden suns as caskets
Have risen, and in their center nine syllables
Are clear; thus the palace of light
Burns all the habitual tendencies toward things,
And the mind, like molten liquid gold,
Shimmers and glistens, blazing with light.
From its dynamic energy, a moon disk;
Above the sun a śrīvatsa blazes,
And upon it rulu radiates and gathers.
This maṇḍala of the śrīvatsa citta
Is clear as a casket of blazing syllables.
This casket of the blazing lotus
Is the place where the garland of syllables coils.
For example, just as from a blazing flower
Nectar-gathering bees take its essence
And collect it into honey,
So from the heart maṇḍala of the śrīvatsa
Flowers of jewel light blaze forth,
And the syllables of rulu swell with essence.
Formed from the yogin's mind,
Many bees that draw out the essence radiate forth;
Soaring out, they draw out the essence of the syllables,
Soaring back, they dissolve it into the casket of the mind.
First, in the color of crystal,
Utterly clear, glistening and blazing,
In the nine places, on the śrīvatsa
At the center of the moon bindus,
The arrangement of syllables becomes clear,
And many crystal bees,
The size of a great garuḍa in flight,
Soar out, draw the essence, and gather back in.
As the syllables blaze with the fire of wisdom,
The heaped firewood of habitual tendencies is burned.
Sent outward, through compassion they exhort the grounds.
Just like the scorching rays of the sun,
They blaze single-pointedly without interruption.
In the middle, in the color of coral,
The arrangement is clear as above,
And many coral bees,
Flying and soaring as above,
Draw out the essence, dissolve it, and radiate once more.
Like a whirling firebrand,
They dissolve into the expanse of the purple śrīvatsa.
The gathering at the very end:
All of the arrangement above
Is transformed into blazing dark blue,
And like great garuḍas swooping on their prey,
Sounding hūṃ, they soar back and forth,
Draw out the essence of rulu, and confer siddhi.
From the center of mindfulness ja is drawn forth,
With blazing hooks and caskets;
Heaped in the center of the śrīvatsa,
The heaps of rulu jewels
Are gathered, drawn in, and dissolved into their place.
The force of their descent, or the blessing,
Is like a great hailstorm rolling down upon a lake.
When joined to the syllables in this way,
It is not the yogin alone:
Even the supreme deities dwelling in the expanse,
Though they are nonreferential in the nature of reality,
When urged by the supreme yogin,
Send forth the radiance of loving compassion,
Like life-force and exertion, like mindful awareness speaking aloud,
Like the voice of the throat and the limbs in motion,
Like syllables blazing as vajra letters,
Down to the vowel hooks, the circles and the dots,
Without blur or haze, unobscured;
Knowledge and awareness expand in the meaning of wisdom.
Even so, by the power of those to be tamed,
One abides at the stage of urging the Victors
And performs the rite that nourishes yoga.
In order to become one with the deity,
Through the count of recitations, through approach,
And through application, one comes close to the goal.
Thus, at the times of practice,
The signs, the warmth, or the marks
That the blessing has come or is near
Arise in the yogin himself, in the substances of accomplishment,
And in all that exists, that is seen, and that is heard.
They are clear in the warmth of the body, in dreams,
And as signs in the mind,
And they arise with certainty in the breath as well.
The body is light as cotton,
And free from the harm of discomfort;
In touch, samādhi is clear;
Even in dreams one becomes intoxicated;
Faith, joy and delight arise;
Yearning bodhicitta arises,
And wonder arises: this is it.
One gains mastery over the moving breath,
Holding it and releasing it just as one wishes.
The substances blaze and leap up,
Fragrance and light arise, and the supreme taste is born;
They increase abundantly without being exhausted;
Sweet, soft and clear, the supreme taste is found.
They harm neither the stomach nor the throat;
When they are eaten, the face is transformed and the body grows robust.
Cymbals sound sweetly, pleasing melodies and forms appear,
And fragrant scents arise: such are the signs.
When the vajra divination images are good,
The signs and marks have come:
Make haste, and take the siddhis.
With the mudrās of the śrīvatsa and the casket,
And with the radiating and gathering of the vajra vase,
Bring the siddhis down into the space of the consort.
Until the higher intention of the union of father and mother
Has come,
The four goddesses of clear wisdom,
With hasta, mirror and bhandha,
With garlands of great fields and the rest,
And with silk streamers of five colors,
Wave them and summon many vidyādharas.
With many musical instruments,
With vajra dance and melodious song,
Relying on all the mātṛkās of existence,
Bring down the great blessing,
And dissolve the siddhis into those who have come.
This is the wheel of vajra song,
The activity of the ornament of wisdom.

Thus spoke Sattvavajra.

From the great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, this is the thirtieth chapter, called vajra song.

Then, again, Sattvavajra spoke this chapter called Entering Vajra Yoga.

The yogin of the Great Assembly,
Gathering together all the sons of noble family of yoga
Who are to enter
Into this sublime secret maṇḍala,
All who have become worthy vessels,
Has them generate the mind of supreme awakening,
And they should remain, speaking these words:
"How wonderful! Into this pleasure grove of supreme bliss
We wish to enter completely.
Having generated the joyful mind of awakening,
We shall practice this secret meaning."
When they have made this request,
The Lord of the Supreme Secret
Purifies their streams and closes the doors of the six realms.
All the yogins who are vidyādharas
Bring forth the great maṇḍala clearly,
And with the mudrās and empowerments
They should purify the streams of saṃsāra.
In the five places of the one who enters,
Having produced the seeds of the six realms,
One should press them down with five hūṃ syllables.
On the crown of the one who has entered,
Fashioned from the wisdom of ba,
A four-petaled lotus: at its center
Place the seeds of humans and gods;
In the east, south, west and north
Plant the seeds of the asuras, the animals, the pretas
And the hells.
With hūṃ the doors of their paths should be closed.
With both hands, press the little fingers with the thumbs,
Join the three fingers and cross them at the forehead:
This is the mudrā of averting, which does not let one go
To the abodes of gods and asuras.
Cross both hands and clasp the armpits:
This is the seal of averting from the human realm.
Make fists, raise the thumbs,
And press the right upon the left:
This is the seal of averting from the abode of animals.
Join the palms evenly and touch the tips of the middle fingers,
Cross the ring fingers behind the middle fingers,
And embrace them from the base with the index fingers:
This is the seal of averting from the realm of the pretas.
Place the back of the right hand upon the left,
And with ring, middle and index fingers, one with another,
Interlock them: this is the seal that closes the door of hell.
Having made these mudrās of the hand,
One utters the vajra seal of speech:
Alas! rudra krodha kyi hūṃ hri samapaca hūṃ hrispaja hūṃ
When this has become clear,
In order to purify the obscurations,
Comes the mudrā of joining in yoga:
"Supreme deity of vajra yoga,
Glorious one who guides the six classes of beings,
Homage to the Vajra Holder!
Protector Maitreya, I go to you for refuge!"
When this supplication has been made,
The one who enters touches with the crown of his head
The very feet of the great yogin.
"Arisen from the three supreme vajra speeches
Of all the buddhas of supreme bliss,
Honored by the three realms of existence,
The great permission of empowerment is bestowed."
atihostahṛdakhaṃ
Then, when they have become vessels,
The secret eye should be opened,
And the deity on whom the flower fell is also given.
Then the great samaya is proclaimed:
"Whoever transgresses this samaya
Of union equal with all the Victors
Has deceived all the Victors.
The hosts of blood-drinking, blazing-mouthed goddesses,
Enraged, will seize his heart blood and drink it."
vajra samaya idanathenarakan varahṛdayanamavastināna samaya rakṣa siddhi vadyi vajra amṛtodakaṃ
Having given the water of the vajra oath to drink,
The great and weighty vajra oath is sworn.
All the vows of vajra samaya,
Each and every one, should be conferred,
And the duties of a disciple should also be conferred.
"O vidyādhara, supreme vajra son,
You are a son of the Victors of yoga.
Do not speak of the secret to others;
If you speak of it, you will burn in hell.
You are Vajra So-and-so,
Son of the Victors, holder of the lineage.
Through the giving of the supreme secret name,
You will enjoy bliss in all the buddha fields."
Through this, one is born in a buddha field.

Thus he spoke. From the great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, this is the thirty-first chapter, called entering vajra yoga.

Then, again, Sattvavajra spoke this chapter on the complete empowerment.

The yogin of the Great Assembly,
Honored by the three realms of existence,
Who abides at the level of the master,
Has the thirty-six supreme secrets:
The outer empowerment and the inner empowerment,
The empowerment of accomplishment and the secret empowerment;
With ten, eleven and thirteen,
They are made complete in two ways.
First among them, this is the empowerment of giving:
oṃ hūṃ sa hrīḥ h
Hard for anyone to transgress,
With mighty words that hold sway over the land,
The empowerment of the mudrā, the king's seal of command:
The five kāyas become complete.
Holding the lineages of each of the families,
The great mark of the symbols
Is the hand emblem of the great union:
The samaya mudrā becomes complete.
The supreme secret of the dhāraṇī of wisdom,
Which has become endowed with meaning,
Which does not go to the two extremes,
Is the great queen of consorts.
When nonduality is completely fulfilled,
All is the state of great bliss,
Joined in meaning with the mind of awakening:
This is the empowerment that completes great wisdom.
The vase of great auspiciousness
Is the palace where the Sugatas dwell;
The great empowerment of the completed maṇḍala
Should be given to him.
So that the secret teaching may spread
And remain for a long time,
The supreme empowerment of the auspicious substances
Should also be given as the great permission.
So that all aims may be accomplished,
With the wish-fulfilling jewel that grants all desires
And the seven precious possessions of royal rule,
He is empowered as supreme king.

A precious lion throne of lotus,
That supreme seat which has become the highest,
Is the empowerment of great distinction:
He should be set upon the great throne.
With diadem, garlands of flowers, and the rest,
He is clothed in the splendid ornaments of supreme empowerment.
Crown ornament, long necklace and short necklace,
Armlets, anklets and bracelets:
The king who has gained empowerment gives these to him.
That king, majestic with the beauty of the empowerment he has gained,
Is a mighty lord, and so all bow down to him.
"He is the Vajra Lord, the King!":
So all should cry out with a great voice and proclaim.
Placing in the hand of that king
A golden wheel with spokes and rim,
This supreme royal empowerment
Of steering the whole world should also be given.
From the time he has become a king who guards the outer circle,
Since he comes to possess the signs of the unsurpassed and supreme,
The secret door should be opened and he should be brought inside.
Having come to possess the most secret substances,
Through the secret door he requests whatever aims he wishes.
It is blessed as the sign of the great permission.
The empowerment to listen to and learn the secret meaning
Should be given to that fortunate one who has gained empowerment.
With this nectar water the secret door of the ears is opened:
sarva tathāgata stamati ho
All the profound volumes and the rest
Should be given to that supreme son of good family.
Since he is able to act for the benefit of beings,
As the sign of accomplishing the whole ocean of activities,
The substances of the four kinds, the crossed vajra and the rest,
Should be given with skill to that supreme son.
That supreme son, who fulfills the hopes and wishes of others,
Just as a king is born in the line of a royal caste,
Has been born as regent in the line of the Dharma;
Therefore the three sublime treasuries, the tantras and the rest, are given.
Because he is able to care for the whole assembly of disciples,
And so that he may abide on the ground of proclaiming the teaching,
This glorious supreme empowerment of the Vajra King
Is given together with the signs and attire of the nine great vehicles.
Since he becomes an unsurpassed king,
The empowerment of seat and throne, supreme sign of having mastered the grounds:
The Great God (Śiva) and the others, the great corpses, together with sun and moon,
Bull, lion, buffalo, leopard and tiger,
Raised up by a fierce bear, should be given.
The signs of being a king are of seven kinds:
The dhāraṇī that holds sway over people and delights the king,
Crushing the armies of others and holding sovereignty,
Possessing the miraculous legs that accomplish the aims of self and others,
And the jewel that fulfills every need and wish like a wish-granting gem:
These should be given with skill to that king.
Since he is a supreme heir of the Victors,
The supreme empowerment of the great seal (mahāmudrā) should be given.
An image of the mudrā, painted on cloth, modeled in relief, or cast in metal,
Should be placed on the crown of his head.
Since he is endowed with the meaning of great wisdom,
With the mudrā, the supreme sign of the five wisdoms,
Touching the five places, the supreme empowerment of mind is given:
oṃ mahā śūnyatā jñāna vajra svabhāva ātmako'haṃ
oṃ mhā ādarśa jña na vajra svabhāva ātmako'haṃ
oṃ mahā samatā jñāna vajra svabhāva ātmako'haṃ
oṃ mahā pratyavekṣaṇa jñāna vajra svabhāva ātmako'haṃ
oṃ mahā kṛtyānuṣṭhana jñāna vajra svabhāva ātmako'haṃ
Since he is the supreme bliss
Of the yoga of vajra body, speech and mind,
This supreme secret of body, speech and mind
Should be given to the Vajra Holder:
oṃ sarva tathāgata kāya vajra svabhāva ātmako'haṃ
oṃ sarva tathāgata vāka vajra svabhāva ātmako'haṃ
oṃ sarva tathāgata citta vajra svabhāva ātmako'haṃ
oṃ sarva tathāgata anurāgaṇa jñāna vajra svabhāva ātmako'haṃ
oṃ sarva tathāgata pūja vajra svabhāva ātmako'haṃ
For that supreme son who has gained the supreme empowerment,
So that all his aims may be auspiciously accomplished,
The supreme auspicious substances that subdue Māra,
Adorned with their emblems, should be given.
When that supreme son who has accomplished all aims
Has gained the supreme vajra empowerment,
The vajra, the drop of mastery,
Renowned throughout all the buddha fields, is given.
Having gained the empowerment of entering within,
The sublime supreme empowerment
To practice and accomplish all of these should be given.
In the unborn expanse of phenomena itself,
Where mind has obtained empowerment,
The empowerment of awareness, of great wisdom,
The decisive key of the meaning by which mind is understood, is given.
The mind of awakening, free of all signs of activity,
Abiding without being sought,
Through great compassion and supreme bliss,
Bestows mastery over all the three realms.
As the sublime supreme empowerment of the sign
Of being able to bring entirely to completion
All the aims of the beings of the three worlds of existence,
A blazing plow is given.
As the great sign of shaking out the three poisons from their depths,
Three amulet boxes,
Filled with the great red,
Are placed in the hand of the one who has gained the great empowerment.
To bestow the great supreme empowerment
Of liberating the three worlds of existence,
Weapons such as the curved knife
Are placed in the hand of that empowered one.
As the great seal of the sign
That whatever one engages in among the phenomena of saṃsāra,
One is not stained by faults,
A lotus of pure substance is given.
As the great sign of having gained the supreme empowerment
In which all activities are brought to completion,
Great staffs of stira
Are placed in the hand of that empowered one.
As the sign of nondual embrace with the consort,
Nondual with the meaning of the nature of reality,
This cluster of coiling black snakes
Is placed in the hand of that empowered one.
A wisdom woman of secret union,
Stainless, utterly pure and lovely to behold,
Endowed with the marks and finely adorned,
Should be given to that empowered one.
Since that great king who has gained empowerment
Is the lord of all,
Music, scents and food,
The five sense pleasures and the rest,
And all the ornaments and articles of enjoyment,
All of them, all, should be given.
To that king of practice,
So that he gains mastery over the activities of practice,
Saying, "Accomplish all the Victors,
Vajrasattva and the rest,
And vajra longevity, medicine and the rest:
Accomplish every practice without exception!",
All the substances for practice should be given.
Because all the victorious buddhas
And that empowered one are one,
The empowerment of the vajra mastery of a vidyādhara,
The vajra name, should be given.
Since he is lord of the empowerment of practice, of the supreme substances,
And of the secret,
The supreme secret empowerment should be given.
The great mind of awakening of joy,
Which satisfies completely,
The remaining white and red mind of awakening
Is placed on the tongue of that empowered one.
The great mind of awakening that is not sought,
Through the power of great wisdom,
Has mastery over the dharmakāya as illusion;
Since this is the empowerment that perfects great mastery,
Even if one has not gained those conventional empowerments
Of substances, words and persons,
If one has gained the mind's empowerment over phenomena,

All the vidyādharas of the Victors
And all the mighty ones of the three worlds of existence
Will confer empowerment upon him.
The measure of empowerment is this:
On one who has faith and reverence,
The great empowerment of hearing the secret is conferred.
If the meaning of the four kinds of activity is complete,
One has gained the empowerment to perform the activities.
If one sees the Dharma through the key point of the view,
One has gained the empowerment to expound with the royal seal.
If one withstands conditions and possesses the three valid means,
One has the empowerment of guru and vajra master.
Knowing how to expound all three sublime treasuries
Without partiality,
One gains the empowerment of king of the boundless.
If one cuts the blindfold cloth for a pretender
Who lacks faith, is dull of mind and very coarse,
It is taught that he is burned by the empowerment.
In one who keeps no secrecy and is forgetful, empowerment does not take hold.
On one who is faithless, lazy and torpid, empowerment does not rub off.
One who is no vessel, devoid of essence, wholly empty,
Has not gained empowerment.
If one who is a fit vessel, with the essence complete, gains empowerment,
That is taught to be the cause of gaining the supreme empowerment.
Thus he spoke.

From the great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, this is the thirty-second chapter, called "The Vajra Empowerment."

Then the vast gathered assembly, having understood that the Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, and the sublime Sattvavajra, together with the great king of tantras, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, are the mirror of all phenomena, were deeply moved and filled with wonder, and spoke this song of praise of exceeding great joy:

How wonderful!
Vanquisher of faults, endowed with excellence, signs stilled, beyond the extremes,
Immeasurable, impartial in love, by your meaning the glory of self and others,
Ultimate, hidden and secret, coiled in the center of space,
Supreme great vajra, mighty lord, master of all,
Free of grasping thought, you revel in flesh, bone and blood;
The cord of your heart's love holds sway to the very ends of saṃsāra;
Objectless and unborn, you are victorious in the battle with signs:
King of supreme bliss, to you I bow and offer praise.
Great wisdom shines as the sun in the sky;
The rays of the mind of awakening dawn in the expanse;
By the force of swift heroism you free the six classes of beings;
Crushing thought, the miraculous displays of the wisdom vajra radiate;
Speaking the sublime meaning, embodiment of the Teacher of nonduality,
Lion of Speech, to you I bow.
Holding precious treasure, you fulfill every wish;
Treasury of all scripture, reasoning and proof,
Clear mirror for those who know the symbols to take up correctly:
The tantra of the Vajra All-Gathering, King Bhairava,
To this mirror of Dharma we bow.

From the great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, this is the thirty-third chapter, the praise of wonder.

The great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, is complete.

Translator's notes

Several names in the assembly could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning (Vajra Regent, Vajra Crow, Vajra Juncture of Time, and the buddhas and yoginīs of the four retinues); Vajra Gophur is left in its Tibetan form because its sense (go phur) is unclear, and rdo rje ging is taken as Vajra Kiṃkara, the ging being a class of attendant dancers. In the description of the palace, ma tang gi gnas and pris ma are obscure: they are taken as "places of outcastes" and "raised mounds"; ga bu, taken as "skulls," and the list of women (slas, byi mo, bran mo, rendered household women, courtesans, and servant women) are also uncertain. The Sanskrit-derived names Saṅa, Bhigśa, and Śorikā are given as the Tibetan spells them. Several retinue names are transliterated Sanskrit-like syllables (Teśa, Tragśa, Ruti, and so on) given here as the Tibetan spells them; their underlying Sanskrit is unclear. Among the secret maidens, "Gathering of Mothers" translates an unidentified name; Pukkasī reads the Tibetan spelling as a variant of the usual form. Among the maidens of joy and the devoted maidens, names that could not be confidently matched to a standard Sanskrit form (Charnel-Bird-Faced, Darkness-Faced, Horse-Faced, Pig-Faced, Monkey-Faced, She Who Grinds to Dust) are translated literally, as are the animal-necked slayers other than Hayagrīva and the vajra slayer goddesses. The second line of the song, literally "from like space, come near to the pledge", is terse and its construction uncertain. The list of plays in the first paragraph contains several rare words (rendered "flirting", "lying close", "chewing", "savoring") whose exact sense is uncertain. In the first song, "you, my lord" may also be read as "glorious lord" addressing Vajrasattva as master. The line "Sugata, maker of the going in bliss" renders a terse phrase playing on the meaning of Sugata, "one gone in bliss". In the list of beings destroyed, several items are unidentified and are translated literally or transliterated: "Craving to Enter" and "Without Craving" may conceal names of Hindu deities, "Bala" renders "the Powerful One", "ludé" is an unknown term, and "fire-bearers" is literal. In Vajrasattva's verse, the Tibetan normally used for the god Gaṇapati is here plainly an epithet of the Buddha being addressed, so it is rendered "Lord of the Assembly". "Grandmother of all awakening minds" renders the Tibetan literally; the sense is "progenitor". The ten examples listed and then expounded do not match their stated correspondents one for one; the text is translated as it stands. "Examining it in a wavering way" renders an obscure word whose sense is uncertain. "The sublime Vajra" at the start appears to be Sattvavajra. The names in "Vajrasattva, the stainless Jewel, and the Giver of Courage; likewise the Vajra Immeasurable and the Various Victor" seem to refer to the buddhas of the five families, but the Tibetan forms are unusual, so they are translated literally. Several lines are terse and their construction uncertain: "To practice afflicted effort where there is no doing is delusion" and "For hell, the great buddha, suffering is the very path of awakening" render obscure verses as literally as sense allows. "The path of the close placements" is read as referring to the mindfulness practices of the śrāvaka path. The passage opens mid-sentence, completing the preceding line: all doing and striving is delusion and a great obstacle to unsurpassed awakening. The phrase ནམ་མཁའི་སྙིང་པོ ("essence of space") here describes the all-ground and is not the bodhisattva Ākāśagarbha. Several rare or uncertain words: གླགས is read as ལོགས ("apart from"); ཐེན་ཚེར is taken as a tiny aperture such as the eye of a needle, as the following lines suggest; ག་ཆད is rendered "undone"; ག་ཞ་མཁན་གྱི་སྤྱོ་འདྲི is taken as "the antics of a jester"; རངས is read as "gratified"; and the line ཤིན་ཏུ་སྐར་མ་རྒྱལ་རྣམས་ལ is obscure and rendered tentatively. རྣལ་འབྱོར་དབང་ཕྱུག is the epithet "lord of yogins" (yogeśvara), not the god Īśvara. A few lines are terse and rendered with some uncertainty: ལོག་རྟོགས་ is taken as "wrong conceptions"; the line གནས་བདེར་ལུས་བཅོས་ཁ་ན་མེད is read as denying any need to adjust the body for a pleasant place, though ཁ་ན་མེད could also mean "unsurpassed"; and the closing lines on "guarding" appear to reinterpret the secrecy of the commitments (samaya) as the mind hidden in emptiness. The verses on the celestial palace, the assembly and the substances reinterpret the outer supports of tantric practice (palace, gaṇacakra gathering, ritual substances) as the mind itself. འདྲེན་མར་ is read as འདྲེས་མར ("intermingled"), which the sense requires. The "eight" signs of warmth and the "three meanings" are not enumerated here and are rendered as they stand. The "two faults, a quiescent body and final cessation" probably refer to the two kinds of nirvāṇa of the lower vehicles, with and without remainder. The closing prose begins a series of names for the mind that continues in the next piece. The text reads སྟོང་པ་ཉིད ("emptiness") in "the Teachers of the present, too, go by this path"; this makes little sense here and is taken as a slip for སྟོན་པ ("Teacher"). In the list of reasons, "great power" (དབང་ཆེན་པོ) corresponds to the name "lord," not to tantric empowerment. Several items in the explanations of the afflictions, causes and conditions are cryptic; "weighed down as by a tree trunk" and "hardened by being unbreakable" are literal renderings of what appear to be images for particular afflictions. The explanations of the twelve conditions and twelve births list eleven items each, and are translated as they stand. The list of conditions names "six awarenesses" with the spelling རིགས་དྲུག (normally "six classes of beings"), but the explanation below calls them རིག་པ་དྲུག, "six awarenesses", so that sense is followed. The six abodes (Endurance, the Mindful, the Equal, Desire, Strife, Tuṣita) are rendered literally; some may be names of world-systems or ages (Strife suggests the Kali age). In the twelve great deeds, the text lists taming Māra twice, and the eighteen wisdoms enumerate only sixteen items without a closing formula; these are translated as they stand. The three modes combine the logician's three characteristics of a valid reason with the imputed and dependent natures. The first paragraph lists the four classic kinds of indirect intention (sameness, another time, another meaning), though here the third is "another enumeration" rather than the usual "the thought of the person"; translated as it stands. The text's own counts differ from the items listed in places (seventeen wisdoms where eighteen were announced, twelve yogas where eleven were announced); the lists are given as they stand. "Tshom ngam chen po" in the modes of arising is obscure; rendered "great majesty" tentatively. In the ten trainings, "'dis bzung" is read as "gyis bzung" (through samaya), and "la ... dor" and "thag ... gcod" are idioms of completing and deciding; rendered closely. "Pa spu" is read as "ba spu", hair. "Wheel of time" here is a cosmic image, not the tantra of that name. The ninth class in the doors of downfall, "shog ma gcan chen," is obscure; "gcan" is read as in "gcan gzan," wild beasts of prey, and "shog ma" is left untranslated as unclear. "The five that are close to them" are the five deeds close to those of immediate retribution; "the four grave ones" and "the eight wrong ones" are given literally. The paradoxical statements that the Buddha's dharmas are "accompanied by" the afflictions and nonvirtues reflect this tantra's nondual view and are translated as they stand. "The Guide" (Tibetan 'dren pa, Skt. nāyaka) is an epithet of the Buddha. "Cang shes gyi ling ngag" is read as the limbs of a thoroughbred horse; the exact sense of "ling ngag" is uncertain. "Mkhar lding" in the garuḍa simile is read as "mkha' la lding," soaring in the sky. "Sbrang ma" (bees, flies) in the ocean simile is rendered "stinging insects." "Guide and lead" (bkri zhing drang ba) may also carry the sense of provisional teaching (drang don, neyārtha) as against the one definitive intent. This passage is dense and terse, and several lines are uncertain. "Chung zhig bshad thabs 'gro" (of the elephants) is rendered "go only a little, as best they can." "Kun du rgyu dang gzhon nu" is taken literally as "wanderer and youth"; these may be the wandering ascetic (parivrājaka) and the youth (kumāra) of some known illustration. "Gnyis kyi chos" ("dharmas of the two") and the following lines on the three that benefit beings are translated literally. "Bcos ma phra men" is read as "contrived and mixed," and "sgrib las zhes rtsi" as "counted as obscurations and actions." The names of the grounds follow the list given earlier in the text, though the order and names here differ slightly from that list. "Rkun mo byi la btags" is read as setting a cat on the thief; here "rkun mo" is the ordinary word "thief" in a simile, not the goddess named Caurī elsewhere in the text. The images of the monkey, the thief, and the empty house are terse and their exact sense uncertain. "Byung tshor," rendered "sudden awareness," denotes the abrupt noticing of what has arisen. "The four of the desire realm" is translated as it stands. "Nam mkha'i snying po" here is "the essence of space," not the bodhisattva Ākāśagarbha. "Appearances" renders ལྟས, literally "omens, portents"; the sense here seems to be the appearing signs from which thought arises. "Mindfulness" in "the stirrings of mindfulness" (དྲན་པ་རྒྱུ་བ) refers to roaming recollection or memory, which the text treats as a fault to be recognized. In "with the mind, instant by instant," the words སེམས་ཀྱི་གྱིན་ད are obscure; I have rendered only the clear sense. "Being one who has reached the ground" renders the terse ས་པ་ཡིན་པས and is tentative. "Sudden awareness" renders བྱུང་ཚོར, as in the preceding passage. "The hips" renders the uncertain སྟ་ཟུར. The syllable there is written ཡི་གེར, which I take as the syllable ra fused with the word "syllable," matching the pattern of the surrounding lines. "The navel, the midpoint of the body" renders སྐུ་ཡི་ཁྲུན་ཚད་ལྟེ་བ, whose first part is obscure. The ranges of breath, scent and hearing measure how far the visualized syllables radiate on each successive ground. "Turns into anything at all" reads ཅིར་ཡང་འགྱུར་ཞིང for the text's ཞིད, an evident slip. The sequence of syllable doors repeats some glosses (for example ṇa and na, ma and ya, e and o share the same door); these are given as the text has them. Tvarana is a Sanskrit word given in Tibetan letters for a class of water creature; I have not been able to identify it with certainty. "Making the yogic salutation with the whole body" renders the obscure phrase རྣལ་འབྱོར་གྱི་ཕྱག་ལུས་ཀུན་དུས་ལ་བརྡབས, which may describe a full prostration. "The Lion of Speech" renders སྨྲ་བའི་སེང་གེ, an epithet of Sattvavajra (possibly Skt. Vādisiṃha). "Prasena divination" renders པྲ, the Tibetan term for mirror and similar divination (Skt. prasena). "The leaves of the vermilion plant" renders རྩི་མཚལ་ལོ་མ, a plant taken as an indicator of gold; the identification is uncertain. "Divination visions (prasena)" renders the Tibetan transliteration "pra," which usually refers to divinatory images seen in a mirror or similar surface; the line could also be read as "signs and pra dream omens." "Good signs" reads the evident bzang po for the written bzar po, a copyist's slip. The three miracles here (magical power, instruction, and revealing the Dharma) loosely follow the classical triad of miracles, but the text's own wording is kept. Several lines are terse and their sense is uncertain: "they will wager their lives to get it by any means" interprets the obscure line about the cut "lifeline" and staking one's life; "ruined both visibly and for a long time" interprets a compressed phrase; "cannot escape grief over it" interprets a difficult final line about the learned one. "Fallen to the lowest" reads the written "fallen from liberation" as the likelier "fallen to the end," matching the later line about falling to the bottom of saṃsāra. The four types of bad vessel are given in the text in four groups of lines. The "elephant Eneya" transliterates an unidentified name; its bezoar (gorocanā) is the valued substance. Several lines in the list of the yogin's disciplines and the samaya summary ("four points of complete equality," "the rejam bird," an unidentified bird known for faithful pairing) are terse and rendered literally. "Secret austerity" reads the written "command" as the similar word for "hardship." The list of samayas (body, speech, mind; not abandoning, accepting, knowing, accomplishing, each fivefold) follows the classic Mahāyoga scheme of root and branch samayas. "White lioness" translates dkar mo here as a description, the familiar image of the snow lioness's milk, rather than as the name Gaurī. "Indifference" renders btang snyoms, elsewhere "equanimity," which here plainly names a fault, a slack neglect. "No ground for any penalty" could also be read "no ground for annihilation." A few lines are terse and rendered literally, notably the "close-knit armor of the vajra fence," the "seat and its nature," and the list of restraints beginning with torpor. The "fields" to be liberated and the "ten fields" are, in this tantric context, the ten kinds of beings that are proper objects of the wrathful rite of liberation. The four great samayas named near the start (nonexistence, openness, singularity, spontaneous perfection) are the classic four samayas of the Great Perfection. The Tibetan code names that follow the second run of syllables (the moons, the chief of life-holders, "gal," the maidservant and attendant, and so on) are secret symbolic names whose referents the text does not state; they are translated literally. "Courage-maker" is a common code word for liquor; the following "kha" is left as written. The gesture code is obscure. "Dbang mo" is taken as the ring finger, which suits the flicking and tasting of nectar at the feast; it could instead mean a female consort in some lines. In "if method raises the hand from the right" the Tibetan "nas" is unclear and may be a scribal error for "sna" (nose) or "rna" (ear). "Mahā, great kiṃkara (ging) and karma" seem to be code names for gestures. "Affection and laughter" translates the unclear "'dza' gad 'drim". "Heroic knowing" translates "dpa' mkhyen", a rite I cannot identify. "Inner treasury" assumes "na spar" should read "nang par". "Great downfalls" reads "ltang chen" as "ltung chen". "The Lord of the World" translates 'jig rten dbang po literally; this may refer to Lokeśvara. The eight bodhisattvas appear only as abbreviations (sa, nam mkha', spyan, rdo rje, byams, rdo rje, kun bzang, dpal). They are read as Kṣitigarbha, Ākāśagarbha, Avalokiteśvara, Vajrapāṇi, Maitreya, Samantabhadra and Mañjuśrī. The second "rdo rje" is left as "Vajra" because the list does not name the eighth figure clearly. "Royal seal of light" reads ཕྱག་རྒྱལ as written; it may be a scribal slip for ཕྱག་རྒྱ, "seal". "The ground of the thirteen" probably means the thirteenth ground, Vajradhara, in the Nyingma system. Several names in this passage cannot be identified with confidence and are translated by their meaning: the aeon All-Arrayed, the Buddha King of Manifest Arising (perhaps Abhyudgatarāja), the yogin Excellent Ornament, the monk Stainless Conduct, the yogin Bliss-Endowed Knower and the woman Excellent One. "Na Zhinépa" is given phonetically because its meaning is unclear. "Pramade" is transliterated as written. The line "One mind is drawn out by another" is obscure. The persons in these stories cannot be identified. Their names are translated by meaning: Giver of Bliss, Pure Intellect, Fearless Intellect, King Holder of Great Clarity, Radiant, Loving One, King of Virtue and Jewel Radiance. "Penaya" is transliterated as written. "The abode of the Thirty-Three gods" translates lha gnas gsum, read as the Trāyastriṃśa heaven. མངོན་སྤྱོད here is read as "those who actually practice" (the king of the following story) rather than "fierce rite," since the story contrasts the practitioner with the one who rejoices in his practice. The brahmin names Kunri and Kaini appear to be sound-spellings and are given phonetically; Virtuous Disciple, Luminous Gift, Virtuous and Luminous are literal renderings of names not confidently identified. Kalyana is given as spelled. The phrase rendered "the armored ranks of emanations in equal troops" (sprul pa'i go tshags ru mnyam) and "the great hunting drive of saṃsāra" (skyas lings) are obscure; the renderings are tentative. "Rites of fulfillment" translates skong, the ritual mending or restoration of commitments. Several lines are obscure, notably "whatever has come upon the turtle's head" and the opening "articles that have come from them" (apparently referring to the Lesser Vehicle people just mentioned); these are rendered literally. "The wheel of the seasons" renders dus kyi 'khor lo, read here as the cycle of periodic offerings rather than the Kālacakra Tantra. The figures for the recitation counts follow a consistent pattern (530,000; 53,000; 5,300; 530) and are read accordingly. The count for the incidental breach, "fifteen, one and three," is given as written; the pattern of the preceding counts (530,000; 53,000; 5,300; 530) might suggest fifty-three. "Supreme bliss" (bde mchog) is taken here as a descriptive term rather than the deity Cakrasaṃvara. The similes "as the moon is hidden by a mountain" and "as a sword's edge is blunted" interpret terse and difficult lines. 'phral ba, in the line on the sign before the desire-family rite, is read as dpral ba, "forehead," since the hand signs follow parts of the body (heart, forehead, tongue, body); this is the evident reading. In the list of the five families only vajra, jewel, lotus and action are named, the first presumably understood. "Birthplace of punishment" reads ཚད་པ in the first question, evidently for ཆད་པ "penalty, punishment," as the text itself writes it in what follows. "The condition of the rite" (ལས་ཀྱི་རྐྱེན) most likely refers to the restoring rites of purification and fulfillment just described, though it could also be read as "a karmic condition." "Continuum of great wisdom" renders ཤེས་རབ་ཆེན་པོའི་རྒྱུད, which could also be taken as "the tantra of great wisdom." The final verses are spoken by the vidyādharas collectively; the first-person singular follows the Tibetan. Several phrases in the confession verses are obscure: ལྟང་འཛིན ("watch over") and རྨའོ་འཚལ ("swore to keep") are taken in their likely senses, and ཞེས་དུས་ནས is read as ཞེས་བྱས་ནས, "having said this." The line ཐུགས་རྗེས་གདུང་འཚོབ་རྡོ་རྗེ་ཐོད་པ་ཅན is taken as the speakers' description of themselves; it could instead address the deity as "Vajra Skull-Bearer, compassionate heir." "Loving protector" (བྱམས་མགོན) is read as an epithet of the Sugata addressed, not as Maitreya. The sattva Vajragarbha (སེམས་དཔའ་རྡོ་རྗེའི་སྙིང་པོ) may be the interlocutor otherwise called Sattvavajra. The five consorts are named with the Dhātvīśvarī pattern (Ākāśa-, Karma-, Dharma-, Ratna- and Vajradhātvīśvarī), reconstructed from the Tibetan "Lady of the Expanse of" space, action, dharma, jewel and vajra. In the earth section the text plays on ས as both "earth" (the last element of the sequence space, wind, fire, water) and "ground" (the ten grounds); "grounds" is used throughout. "Taming on the nine grounds" (ས་དགུ་འདུལ་བའི་བྱེ་བྲག) is obscure. "Four gatekeepers" reads སྒོམ་བཞི as སྒོ་མ་བཞི. "Upaya" is the class of tantra between Kriyā and Yoga (often Ubhaya or Caryā). "Ten sorceresses brandishing staffs" reads ཕྲ་མེན་མ་བཅུ་ད་བྱུག་པ as བཅུ་དབྱུག་པ, matching the parallel phrases. "Swift-goers" translates ཤུགས་འགྲོ, a class of goddess not identified with confidence. "Liberation" (སྒྲོལ་བ) is the tantric term for liberating beings through wrathful means. "Fields" (ཞིང) in the first verse is the technical term for beings who are proper objects of wrathful liberation. "Bhandha" (the Sanskrit word as spelled in the Tibetan) here denotes the skull or cranium of the corpse. "Before the men's hands have lost their warmth" is a tentative reading of ལག་ཚོད་མ་ཡལ་བར; the sense is "in no time at all." This section describes the classical types of women (lotus, conch, elephant, marked) as consorts for the practice of union; several physiognomic terms are rare and their renderings are tentative, in particular "sog ka" (taken as shoulders), "kos ko" (taken as cheeks), "rlung kar lci" (rendered "its breath heavy"), "gel bag" (crooked manner) and "rtsid mgo" (coarse-haired head). "The woman with markings" (ri mo can) and "the elephant woman" (ban glang) likely correspond to the Citriṇī and Hastinī types, and "the lotus woman" and "the conch woman" to Padminī and Śaṅkhinī, but I have translated the Tibetan descriptively. "Twelve, twenty-five" is probably an age reference; "mula rakta" (root blood) is left as written. "Sattvavajra" here and "the sattva Vajragarbha" in the same passage appear to refer to the same speaker. "There is no dread of transference and change" renders the line ending ཉམ་མིང, which is a syllable short and unclear as written; I have read it as ཉམ་ང་མེད "without dread", which fits the verse's pattern of negations. The coded words exchanged in the rite of drawing in are kept as written. "Union and liberation" (sbyor sgrol) are the tantric practices of sexual union and ritual "liberation". The swaying profusion of offerings (ling long) is a tentative rendering. "The sublime nine openings" renders dgu lung, which is obscure; "nine openings" (the bodily orifices) is a tentative reading. "Charnel-ground beasts" renders dur gcan, and "desolate gorge" renders ngam grog bshung; both are tentative. The "Loving One" (byams ldan) is kept as earlier in the text. Several lines are compressed and their renderings tentative: "sacred essences that liberate" (bcu dam thar pa), "skull cups together with braided locks" (ka pā la dang lcang lor bcas), "cutting the trajectory with waves of qualities", "the great secret time of the corpse" (bam chen), and the line about the fox (wa), whose image of driving off impure provisions with the fox's cry or whistle is uncertain. Kokila is kept as the name of a musical instrument. "Great sovereign lord" renders dbang phyug che, said here of one's own nature. The goddess addressed as the one who "knows and bears witness" appears to be the earth goddess, invoked as witness when the site is claimed. "Lcepok" is an unidentified plant used for fumigation. The intruding spirits and samaya-breaking sri demons render 'jug srid and dam sri; the phramen are the animal-headed female attendants of the charnel-ground maṇḍala. "Matraṃ" is the name of the demon Rudra in Nyingma myth. Several descriptive lines (the "five tastes", the pastes "in the manner of the wisdom mother's flowing") are tentative. The line "a yaṃ ja laṃ khaṃ" ends with the Tibetan word lhan, read here as "together"; it may instead be part of the seed-syllable run. The phrase dri chen in "drive off the strong odor of the various burnt offerings" is taken in its sense of "strong smell" (of the sur offerings) rather than the ritual sense "excrement," which does not fit the construction. "The Great Glorious Invincible One" (dpal chen mi pham) is an epithet that could not be identified with confidence as a specific Sanskrit name and is translated literally. "Yal dam" in the vajra pledge line is obscure; rendered by context as "pledge." "Bearing of a heroine" renders sems ma, whose exact sense here is not certain. The line about drawing the cord against "bhūtas and vile beings" is grammatically loose in the Tibetan; it is read as the protective boundary set against obstructing spirits. "Disruptions" renders 'drul, possibly for 'khrul. Kacali is kept as written; it appears to be a material (perhaps a kind of cloth) on which the deities are drawn. "Sixty-four" reads rtsa zhi as rtsa bzhi. "Melody of Vajra Ash" is a literal rendering of a name that could not be identified with a Sanskrit form. The lines on the seat ("the long human substance of a person," "the pipar cushion") are obscure and rendered literally. "Blood Snatcher" renders the name khrag 'phrog ma literally; it could not be identified with confidence. Kāśyapa of the Matted Locks renders 'od srung ral pa can. The line "with practices of abstaining from passion" reads chags pa'i spyod pa in context as ascetic restraint; it may simply mean "passionate conduct." "Empty in seven ways" is literal; the seven are not specified. "Is a load carried as a burden" renders khur du bshal ba'i gdos, whose exact sense is unclear. "A great food of rank stench" renders dku ba'i rngad zas, also uncertain. "The great predators that haunt the toll gates" renders sho gam gnas kyi gcan chen, a class of spirits not identified with confidence. Several ritual terms are obscure and rendered literally: "the great crystal, the tsara" (shel chen tsa ra), "the manner of drawing forth" ('jo tshul, possibly a manner of gait), "the vajra rites of bra", "great majesty" (tsham ngam chen po), and "the gaping maw" (kha long kha). "The one with vajra breath", "rolled pellet" (sgril ma) and "a span in size" (mtshon gang / tshon gang) follow the most likely readings. The passage describes a liberation rite and the subsequent examination of signs on the effigy or remains; the speaker of the lines about "me, the lord of desire" is taken as the narrator of the dialogue. This passage lists the types and signs of skull cups used in practice. The Sanskrit-looking type names (paṃcakula, naśa, mahākesa, āyuḥphala, mele debyi, khasvakha, khahupa, jñānamurtti, jñānakuṇḍali, kuṇḍali, ekajaṭi, abimuka, putra) are given as spelled; several are not securely identifiable. "Fulfills all hopes" reads skor as skong. The line "increase of the white with its accompaniments, and the head not appearing" (bcas dkar 'phel dang mgo mi 'byung) is obscure. "Slender Sapling" (lcug phran) is kept as in earlier pieces, though here it may simply name a skull type. Several terms of the maṇḍala construction are obscure: "compartments" (le tshe), "eight facing inward on the spokes" (reading rtsi bsal as rtsibs), and "the white and the red in union". "The mighty crescent moons" renders dbang chen zla gam. "The vajra song of changing calls" renders the obscure khyud skad. The line on the nine expanses of a single bhaga and "nine closed, one opened" is cryptic and rendered literally. "Armies" reads dbung as dpung. Several terms are obscure and rendered literally or left as spelled: "the yatha" (ya tha), "lastly the servants" (tha ma bran), "the tsara of liberation" (thar pa tsa ra), "śi ne da hu", "beyond the hu and below" ('da' hu man 'das), "black tongue incense" (lce pog nag po), "Viṣṇu amulet box", and "bra" (also met in the earlier "vajra rites of bra"). "Divination" renders pra, as in the "vajra diviner" (rdo rje pra mkhan), i.e. prasena-type divination. "The lesser supreme" (mchog phran) in the seventeenth chapter title is uncertain. "Mouth divination" renders kha pra. "Mere trifling causes and conditions" renders gyin da tsam, which is obscure. The asura figure is called the Gaṇapati (tshogs bdag) of the asuras, named Sgyu ma mkhan, rendered as Māyākāra; he is then addressed as "Great Gaṇapati". "The sky offering" reads hom as homa. "Thirteen measures" supplies an unstated unit. Several ritual lines in chapter twenty are obscure and rendered literally: "make the charnel ground with a head as well" (dur khrod mgo ma yang du bya), "from the four, those four are praised in turn" (bzhi las de bzhis chag res bstod), "ramparts, dry and moist" (skam dang ra ri rlon pas, ra ri read as an enclosing ridge), and "coils of guggulu and latticework, corpses" (gu gu thul dang za ra tshags, zhing; gu gu read as guggulu, zhing as the tantric term for a corpse). "Spaces between the spokes" reads rtsibs mchan as rtsibs mtshams. "Emissaries" renders phyag brnyan (servants, attendants). "Mātaṅga" renders ma tang, an outcaste figure. "Lakchuhwa" (lag chu hwa) is unidentified, apparently a kind of fire or a fiery substance, and is kept in phonetics. "Half-sized platforms" renders the obscure chag phyed. In the "six" and "eight" of Matraṃ's body, mtshan is read as the genitals (four limbs, head and genitals make six; with the two secret openings, eight). The final verses of the piece continue into the next passage. Several of the medicinal ingredients are uncertain: "fragrant kha" (kha dri zhim), "white chebok" (lce bog dkar) and "ligadur" (li ga dur) are not securely identified and are given literally or in phonetics. "Myrobalan" renders a ru ra (harītakī); "turuṣka" renders du ru ska. "Bring down and invite" (dbab cing drang) takes the vidyādharas as invoked beings. "Bathing and confession" reads deng as dang. "Nectar paste" renders skyo ma (a thick liquid preparation) and "nectar dough" renders lde gu. "King of medicines, or something close to it" reads ram nye ba as the particle "or" plus "close"; it could instead name an unidentified plant. "Keep apart for a year" renders the obscure lo yis bshugs te. "The five glorious auspicious ones" is given literally; their identity is not specified. "A year's span" renders lo yi skad cig, literally "a year-instant," apparently a measured period of practice. "Dissolved into oneself" reads rang nyid sa as rang nyid la. "Five aggregates" reads lung bo as phung po. The colophon here calls the work a "tantra" (rgyud) rather than a "sūtra." The line "holding the limit of purity, holding the supreme" (dag pa'i mtha' 'dzin nyes mchog 'dzin) is obscure and rendered tentatively. "Stakes" renders སྡོང་བུ, here evidently the posts or sticks on which the cloths bearing heart mantras are hung. ལྟང, rendered "tallies," is uncertain; the context suggests the official who keeps count of the ritual actions with tablets (white for the 108 activities, black for the deceased's evil deeds, which are burned). "The White One" (དཀར་མོ) refers back to Pāṇḍaravāsinī. In the chapter on mudrās, A and MA appear to stand for the two hands (means and wisdom), and their "ten seeds" for the ten fingers. "The heavy bird" renders རྨུན་བྱ, a bird I cannot identify with confidence; the name is translated literally. The line "This is the cause of becoming the essence" follows the text's རྒྱུ "cause"; it may be a slip for རྒྱ "seal," which the parallel lines use. Several terms in the chapter on vajra song are technical names for vocal techniques and melodies in Tibetan chant that I cannot securely identify; they are given in phonetics: tenkö (བསྟེན་ཀོས), jimgö (འཇིམ་གོས), birkwa (འབིར་ཀྭ), drenkö (འདྲེན་ཀོས), and the six songs Shatapa, Gartapa, Changkadeu, Bangkadeu, Drengngewa and Dungngewa. "The drawn-out tone" and "the white tone" render རྒྱངས and དཀར, likewise uncertain. "Offering of the feast" renders སྟན་འགན, a doubtful expression. "Rouses the elements of the corpse" could also mean "rouses the elements of taste" (རོ); the funerary context of the preceding chapters favors "corpse". "Full moon" and maṃsa "meat" are coded names of the sacramental substances. "At the times of practice" reads སྦྱོར་བ "application" for the text's སྟོར་བ "losing," which does not fit. "The mudrās of the śrīvatsa" reads བེའུ for the text's བེད, which yields no clear sense here; this is conjectural. hasta "hand" and bhandha "skull cup" are among the goddesses' implements; "great fields" (ཞིང་ཆེན) is a tantric code term for human flesh or corpses. The line "One abides at the stage of urging the Victors" takes the obscure གཞེས as "abide" from context. བདེ་མཆོག is rendered "supreme bliss" rather than as the name Cakrasaṃvara, since in this Nyingma context it qualifies the pleasure grove and the buddhas generally. ba in "the wisdom of ba" is read as a seed syllable; the line is obscure. "Thumbs" renders འབྲུ, here evidently a finger. "Vajra So-and-so" renders ཆེ་གེ་མོ, the placeholder for the secret name given to the initiate. Here བདེ་མཆོག is read as "supreme bliss," a quality of the yoga and of compassion, not the deity Cakrasaṃvara. ཞིང་ཆེན ("great field") is taken in its tantric sense of human corpses forming part of the throne. སྟི་ར in "staffs of stira" is unidentified (possibly Sanskrit sthira, "firm"); it is kept as a sound. ཀ་ཏར is taken as the curved knife (kartari). "The great red" (དམར་ཆེན) is the tantric substance of blood (rakta). རྒྱལ་ཐེབ is uncertain; it is rendered "royal seal," though it may be a slip for རྒྱལ་ཚབ, "regent," which appears earlier in this rite. "Cuts the blindfold cloth" takes རས་གཅོད as the ritual removal of the blindfold during the empowerment. བདེ་མཆོག is read as "supreme bliss," an epithet of the one praised, not the deity Cakrasaṃvara. The subject of "having understood" is taken to be the assembly, marked by the instrumental.
